Based on the repository structure and file summaries provided, here's a high-level map of the bilt repository:

## Repository Structure

### Packages and Modules
- **`src/bilt/`** - Main package containing core functionality
  - `core/` - Core modeling and system definition
  - `symbolic/` - Symbolic computation and expression handling  
  - `units/` - Unit handling and conversion
  - `_backend/` - Backend execution systems (JAX, Python)

### Core Abstractions

**System Modeling:**
- `Interface` - System interface definition using metaclass
- `Implementation` - System implementation registering components
- `BasicEquation`/`equation` decorator - Equation definitions returning symbolic trees
- `BasicTransform`/`transform` decorator - Transform definitions

**Symbolic Computation:**
- `Tree` - Hash-consed immutable symbolic tree representation
- `Atom` - Symbolic leaf values with hash-consing
- `Expr` - Symbolic expressions
- `SubSystemExpr` - Expressions for sub-system references

**Execution:**
- `Backend` protocol - Execution backend interface
- `JAXBackend`, `PythonBackend` - Concrete backend implementations
- `EvaluationContext` - Context management for evaluations
- `AnalysisPlan`, `PlanStep` - Structural analysis for execution planning

### Dependencies Between Components

```
Interface → Implementation → Components (Equations/Transforms) → Backend Execution
    ↑           ↓
System     SubSystemConstituent (references other Interfaces)
    ↑           ↓
Symbolic   → Tree/Expr → Equations return symbolic relations
    ↑           ↓
Units      → Quantity/Unit system for dimensional analysis
```

### Main Evaluation/Data Flow

1. **Definition Phase**: 
   - Define interfaces using `Interface` metaclass
   - Implement systems with `Implementation` registering equations/transforms
   - Equations return symbolic relation trees (`Tree` objects)

2. **Analysis Phase**:
   - Structural analysis determines required inputs for outputs
   - `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` finds solution plans

3. **Execution Phase**:
   - `BackendCallable` created from analysis plan
   - Inputs processed through backend (JAX/Python)
   - Results returned with proper dimensional handling

### Important Public API Entry Points

- `@equation` decorator for defining equations
- `@transform` decorator for defining transforms  
- `Implementation` class for system registration
- `Interface` metaclass for system definition
- Backend selection via context managers (`with` blocks)
- `EvaluationRequest` for specifying what to compute

### Files That Need Full Inspection

The repository map is insufficient without these key files:
1. **Backend implementations** (`src/bilt/_backend/`)
2. **Structural analysis** (`src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/`)  
3. **System composition** (`src/bilt/core/_sub_system.py`)
4. **Evaluation context management** (`src/bilt/core/_evaluation_context.py`)
5. **Symbolic tree operations** (`src/bilt/symbolic/_tree.py`, `_walk.py`)
6. **Unit system** (`src/bilt/units/`)

The current file summaries show the API structure but don't reveal the complete implementation details of how nested systems are composed and executed. To fully understand systems-of-systems, I would need to see the backend execution flow, the full structural analysis implementation, and how sub-system references are resolved during evaluation.

## Systems-of-Systems Implementation Details

Based on the available information, systems of systems are implemented through:

1. **Interface Composition**: 
   - `Interface` metaclass allows defining system interfaces
   - Interfaces can reference other interfaces as parameters (sub-systems)
   - `SubSystemConstituent` handles references to other implementations

2. **Equation Sub-System Parameters**:

   - The `equation` decorator extracts these and builds symbolic representations
   - `SubSystemExpr` represents how equations reference ports of sub-systems

3. **Implementation Registration**:
   - `Implementation` class registers components including sub-systems
   - Internal implementations can be provided for nested systems
   - Validation ensures proper interface matching between components

4. **Execution Flow**:
   - Structural analysis determines how to connect systems
   - Backends execute the composed system through chained calls
   - Evaluation context manages dimensional consistency across systems

Based on the additional files provided, here's an enhanced analysis of the bilt repository:

## Enhanced Repository Map

### Core System Architecture Files:
- **`src/bilt/core/_model_constituent.py`** - Protocol defining model components (equations/transforms)
- **`src/bilt/core/_port.py`** - Port handling with dotted naming and mangling for backend compilation
- **`src/bilt/core/_domain_registry.py`** - Manages dimensional bindings during evaluation
- **`src/bilt/core/_canonical_ordering.py`** - Handles canonical domain ordering for consistent tensor operations

### Symbolic System Files:
- **`src/bilt/symbolic/_atoms.py`** - Hash-consed atom system for symbolic values (Symbols, Ops, Relations)
- **`src/bilt/symbolic/_dimensions.py`** - Symbolic dimension definitions and coordinate axes
- **`src/bilt/symbolic/_domains.py`** - Domain handling for symbolic expressions with ordered dimensions
- **`src/bilt/symbolic/_expressions.py`** - High-level expression API building on Tree objects

### Unit System Files:
- **`src/bilt/units/_dimensions.py`** - Physical dimension definitions with caching
- **`src/bilt/units/_quantities.py`** - Quantity handling with unit conversion
- **`src/bilt/units/_units.py`** - Unit definitions, composition, and system

## Systems-of-Systems Implementation

### Key Components for Nested Systems:

1. **Interface Composition**:
   - Interfaces can reference other interfaces as parameters (sub-systems)
   - `SubSystemConstituent` handles references to other implementations
   - `Implementation` class manages registration of components including sub-systems

2. **Equation Sub-System Parameters**:
   - Equations can have interface-typed parameters representing sub-systems
   - The `equation` decorator extracts these and builds symbolic representations
   - `SubSystemExpr` represents how equations reference ports of sub-systems

3. **Implementation Registration**:
   - `Implementation` class registers components including sub-systems
   - Internal implementations can be provided for nested systems
   - Validation ensures proper interface matching between components

4. **Execution Flow**:
   - Structural analysis determines how to connect systems
   - Backends execute composed systems through chained calls
   - Evaluation context manages dimensional consistency across systems

### Data Flow in Systems-of-Systems:

1. **Definition Phase**: 
   - Define interfaces using `Interface` metaclass
   - Implement systems with `Implementation` registering equations/transforms
   - Equations reference sub-systems via interface parameters

2. **Analysis Phase**:
   - `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` finds solution plans through bipartite graph search
   - Handles cross-system dependencies and routing
   - Validates that all required inputs can be computed

3. **Execution Phase**:
   - `BackendCallable` created from analysis plan
   - Inputs processed through backend (JAX/Python)
   - Results returned with proper dimensional handling
   - Domain registry ensures consistent dimension bindings across nested systems

### Key Insights:

The system uses a **graph-based approach** where:
- Systems are represented as nodes in a bipartite graph
- Components (equations/transforms) are constituent nodes  
- Available inputs and desired outputs are attribute nodes
- The analysis searches for paths through this graph to compute desired outputs

The **sub-system handling** works by:
- Recognizing interface-typed parameters in equations
- Creating `SubSystemConstituent` entries that reference other implementations
- Using structural analysis to determine how sub-systems can be satisfied

No changes needed to existing files as they represent the complete current API structure. The repository shows a well-designed system for composing systems with proper dimensional analysis and backend execution support.

Based on my analysis of the bilt repository, here are some architectural improvements that could be made:

## 1. **Enhanced Sub-System Composition**
- **Problem**: Current sub-system handling is basic - systems can reference other interfaces but composition is limited
- **Improvement**: Add support for hierarchical system composition where sub-systems can themselves contain nested systems
- **Implementation**: Extend `SubSystemConstituent` to handle recursive system definitions

## 2. **Improved Backend Abstraction**
- **Problem**: Backend interface is somewhat basic and doesn't fully leverage modern execution patterns
- **Improvement**: Add support for asynchronous execution, caching strategies, and more sophisticated compilation optimizations
- **Implementation**: Extend `Backend` protocol with async methods and caching interfaces

## 3. **Better Error Handling and Diagnostics**
- **Problem**: Error messages during system analysis could be more informative
- **Improvement**: Add detailed diagnostic information for failed analysis attempts
- **Implementation**: Enhance `BiltSystemAnalysisError` with context information about why paths fail

## 4. **Enhanced Tensor/Dimension Support**
- **Problem**: Dimension handling is good but could support more complex tensor operations
- **Improvement**: Add better support for tensor broadcasting, more sophisticated alignment strategies
- **Implementation**: Extend `DomainRegistry` and alignment systems with more advanced features

## 5. **Modular System Validation**
- **Problem**: Validation happens at registration time but could be more modular
- **Improvement**: Add pluggable validation hooks for different system types
- **Implementation**: Create validation registry that can be extended by users

## 6. **Improved Documentation and Type Hints**
- **Problem**: Some complex type relationships aren't fully expressed
- **Improvement**: Add more comprehensive type hints and documentation
- **Implementation**: Enhance docstrings and add more precise typing where needed

## 7. **Enhanced Configuration Management**
- **Problem**: No clear way to manage system configurations or parameters
- **Improvement**: Add configuration management for system parameters and settings
- **Implementation**: Create configuration objects that can be passed to implementations

## 8. **Better Testing Infrastructure**
- **Problem**: While there are tests, the testing infrastructure could be more comprehensive
- **Improvement**: Add more sophisticated test fixtures and mocking capabilities
- **Implementation**: Extend test utilities for system composition scenarios

These improvements would make the system more robust, extensible, and easier to use for complex system modeling scenarios.

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user is asking for architectural recommendations for the bilt repository. Let me think carefully about what I've seen in the code and what genuine architectural issues or opportunities exist.

Let me analyze the code I've seen:

1. **System/Implementation model**: `Interface` is a metaclass, `Implementation` registers components. The `Implementation.__init__` does a lot: builds sub-system constituents, validates them, creates a mapping proxy, and registers itself. The `evaluate` method does domain binding, lowering, alignment, backend execution, and lifting all in one method.

2. **Backend system**: `Backend` is a Protocol with `transpile`, `chain_case`, `apply_alignment`, and context manager methods. `InProcessPythonMixin` does AST transformation and compilation. `JAXBackend` adds JIT compilation. The `chain_case` in JAXBackend builds a string and re-parses it as AST - this is fragile.

3. **Structural analysis**: `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` does bipartite graph search with retry logic. The `BipartiteGraph` uses networkx. The search is DFS with exclusion sets.

4. **Symbolic system**: `Tree` is hash-consed, `Atom` is hash-consed, `Expr` is a thin wrapper. The `Expr.__eq__` returns a Tree (relation), which is unusual but intentional.

5. **Units system**: Very comprehensive with `Unit`, `UnitComposition`, `UnitOrder`, `Quantity`, `Dimension`. Heavy use of caching via `__new__`.

6. **Domain/Dimension system**: `Dim` is a simple named dimension, `Domain` is an ordered collection, `DomainRegistry` manages bindings during evaluation.

Let me think about genuine architectural issues:

**Issue 1: JAXBackend.chain_case uses string building + re-parsing**
The `chain_case` method in `JAXBackend` builds a Python function as a string, then parses it back to AST. This is fragile, hard to debug, and bypasses the AST transformation pipeline. The `PythonBackend` does it differently with a closure. This inconsistency is a problem.

**Issue 2: Implementation.evaluate is doing too much**
The `evaluate` method handles: domain binding, input lowering, alignment, backend execution, output lifting, and domain binding of output. This is a lot of responsibility. It would be cleaner to separate these concerns.

**Issue 3: The bipartite graph search is O(n) retry-based**
The `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser.analyse` method does a while loop that retries with excluded steps. This is essentially a backtracking search but implemented as repeated full searches. For large systems this could be very slow.

**Issue 4: Sub-system handling is ad-hoc**
The `_build_sub_system_constituents` and `_validate_sub_systems` functions in `_implementation.py` do string-based name matching. The `SubSystemConstituent` is referenced but I haven't seen its full implementation. The relationship between `BasicEquation.__bilt_sub_systems__` and `SubSystemConstituent` is implicit.

**Issue 5: The Backend protocol mixes concerns**
`Backend` has `transpile`, `chain_case`, `apply_alignment`, and context manager methods. Alignment is a tensor concern, not a backend concern. The `NDArrayAlignmentMixin` is a workaround.

**Issue 6: No clear separation between "model definition" and "model execution"**
The `Implementation` class mixes registration (construction) with execution (`evaluate`, `get_evaluation`). The `EvaluationContext` is created inside `evaluate` but the domain registry is part of it.

**Issue 7: The units system is very heavy**
The `Unit` class has extensive caching, multiple factory methods (`__new__`, `new_named`, `new_absolute`), and complex equality. This is a lot of machinery. The `UnitComposition` also has heavy caching. This could be simplified.

**Issue 8: No clear extension points**
There's no plugin system for custom backends, custom analysers, or custom validation. The `StructuralAnalyser` protocol exists but there's no registry.

**Issue 9: The symbolic system has a leaky abstraction**
`Expr.__eq__` returns a `Tree`, not a `bool`. This is documented but it means `Expr` can't be used in normal Python contexts (dicts, sets, conditionals). The `__bool__` raises `TypeError` which is good but the overall design is unusual.

**Issue 10: No versioning or migration path**
The system has no concept of versioning for interfaces or implementations. If an interface changes, there's no way to handle backward compatibility.

Looking at the code carefully, here are the architectural changes I'd recommend, ordered by impact:

---

## 1. Replace string-based AST construction in `JAXBackend.chain_case`

**File:** `src/bilt/_backend/_jax/_backend.py`

The `chain_case` method builds a Python function as a string, then re-parses it with `ast.parse`. This is fragile (breaks on parameter names that aren't valid identifiers, bypasses the AST transformer pipeline, and is hard to debug). `PythonBackend.chain_case` avoids this by using a closure. The two backends should share a common chaining strategy.

**Recommendation:** Build the `ast.FunctionDef` programmatically (using `ast.FunctionDef`, `ast.Call`, `ast.Return` nodes) rather than via string interpolation. This keeps both backends consistent and ensures the JAX AST transformer (`JAXASTTransformer`) is applied to the chained function, not just the individual transforms.

---

## 2. Separate concerns in `Implementation.evaluate`

**File:** `src/bilt/core/_system/_implementation.py`

`evaluate` currently does five distinct things in sequence:
1. Domain binding of inputs
2. Input lowering (`lower_inputs`)
3. Tensor alignment (`generate_alignment_plan` + `apply_alignment`)
4. Backend execution (`backend_callable.evaluate`)
5. Output lifting (`lift_output`) + domain binding of output

These are independent concerns. A failure in step 3 gives no context about which step failed. The method also mixes the "output is already in inputs" fast path with the full pipeline.

**Recommendation:** Extract a pipeline of named stages (e.g., `LoweringStage`, `AlignmentStage`, `ExecutionStage`, `LiftingStage`) that `evaluate` composes. Each stage is independently testable and can report which stage failed. The fast path (`output in inputs`) should be a separate early return before the pipeline.

---

## 3. Replace the retry loop in `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser.analyse`

**File:** `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_exhaustive/_backend.py`

The `while True` loop with an accumulating `excluded` frozenset is backtracking implemented as repeated full DFS searches. For a system with *n* alternative routes, this is O(n) full graph traversals. The `BipartiteGraph.search` already does DFS with a `visited` set, so the outer loop is redundant work.

**Recommendation:** Make `BipartiteGraph.search` return an iterator of all valid plans (or accept a callback), so the analyser can try the next plan without restarting the search. Alternatively, if the graph is small enough that the current approach is fine, at minimum add a bound on retries and a clearer error message listing all attempted routes.

---

## 4. Make sub-system composition an explicit API

**Files:** `src/bilt/core/_system/_implementation.py`, `src/bilt/core/_sub_system.py`

Currently, sub-systems are discovered implicitly: an equation parameter annotated with an `Interface` type is detected by `_sub_system_parameters`, and `SubSystemConstituent` objects are built from `internal_implementations`. The relationship between `BasicEquation.__bilt_sub_systems__` and `SubSystemConstituent` is implicit and validated by string-name matching in `_validate_sub_systems`.

**Recommendation:** Add an explicit `compose` or `sub_system` API on `Implementation` (or a decorator) that makes the parent-child relationship a first-class construct. This would:
- Replace string-name matching with type-level checks
- Allow a sub-system to be composed without requiring an equation to reference it
- Make the dependency graph visible at construction time rather than at analysis time

---

## 5. Decouple alignment from the backend protocol

**Files:** `src/bilt/_backend/_base_backend.py`, `src/bilt/_backend/_ndarray_alignment_mixin.py`

`Backend` (a `Protocol`) requires `apply_alignment`, but alignment is a tensor concern, not a backend concern. `NDArrayAlignmentMixin` is a workaround that both `PythonBackend` and `JAXBackend` inherit. A future backend that doesn't use NumPy arrays would still be forced to implement or inherit this.

**Recommendation:** Move `apply_alignment` out of the `Backend` protocol. Make it a separate `AlignmentStrategy` that `Implementation.evaluate` (or the pipeline from recommendation 2) applies before calling the backend. The backend only sees already-aligned inputs.

---

## 6. Make `DomainRegistry` immutable after binding

**File:** `src/bilt/core/_domain_registry.py`

`DomainRegistry` is mutable and uses a `_locked_against_new_dimensions` flag to prevent late bindings. This is a code smell: the object is in a "half-built" state during evaluation. The `lock` call is easy to forget, and the error only surfaces at the next `bind` call, not at the point where the lock was needed.

**Recommendation:** Split into a `DomainBinder` (mutable, used during input binding) and a `DomainRegistry` (frozen, used during execution). The transition is explicit: `registry = binder.freeze()`. This makes the two phases visible in the type system.

---

## 7. Add a backend/analyser registry

**Files:** `src/bilt/_backend/__init__.py`, `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/__init__.py`

`StructuralAnalyser` is a `Protocol` but there's no registry. `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` is the only implementation and is hard-coded in `Implementation.analyser`. Similarly, backends are singletons (`JAX_BACKEND`, `PYTHON_BACKEND`) with no discovery mechanism.

**Recommendation:** Add a simple registry (a `dict[str, type]` or a `@register` decorator) so users can plug in custom analysers or backends without modifying core code. This is the most natural extension point for the system.

---

## 8. Simplify the units caching layer

**Files:** `src/bilt/units/_units.py`, `src/bilt/units/_dimensions.py`

`Unit`, `UnitComposition`, `UnitOrder`, and `Dimension` all use `__new__`-based caching with class-level `_cache` dicts. This is four separate caching layers with subtle interactions (e.g., `Unit.__new__` can return a cached `UnitOrder.unit` directly). The `Unit` class has three factory paths (`__new__`, `new_named`, `new_absolute`) that each set attributes differently.

**Recommendation:** Consolidate into a single `UnitRegistry` that owns all caching. `Unit` becomes a thin frozen dataclass. This removes the `# noqa: SLF001` suppressions (private attribute access from outside the class) and makes the caching strategy testable in isolation.

---

## Summary of priority

| Priority | Change | Impact |
|----------|--------|--------|
| High | #1 JAX chain_case | Correctness / maintainability |
| High | #2 evaluate pipeline | Debuggability / testability |
| Medium | #3 Analyser retry loop | Performance for large systems |
| Medium | #4 Explicit composition | API clarity |
| Medium | #5 Alignment decoupling | Backend extensibility |
| Low | #6 DomainRegistry immutability | Safety |
| Low | #7 Registry | Extensibility |
| Low | #8 Units simplification | Maintainability |

Good, I now have the full `SubSystemConstituent` implementation. Here's what I still need to complete the architectural picture:

## Critical files to add

| File | Why |
|------|-----|
| `src/bilt/core/_realize_analysis/_realize.py` | The bridge between structural analysis and backend execution. `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser.analyse` calls `realize_plan` to turn a `PlanStep` sequence into a `BackendCase`. Without this, the execution pipeline is opaque. |
| `src/bilt/core/_system/_decorator.py` | Implements the `@system` decorator — the primary user-facing entry point for defining a system. Shows how `Implementation` is constructed and how `internal_implementations` are wired. |
| `src/bilt/core/_system/_lowering_and_lifting.py` | The `lower_inputs` / `lift_output` / `generate_lifting_plan` functions called in `Implementation.evaluate`. Needed to understand the full evaluation pipeline. |
| `src/bilt/core/_evaluation_context.py` | The `EvaluationContext` that holds the `DomainRegistry` and is created per-evaluation. Needed to understand the lifecycle of domain bindings. |
| `src/bilt/core/__init__.py` | Shows what is actually exported as the public API (e.g., whether `system`, `equation`, `transform`, `Implementation` are all re-exported). |

## Important but secondary

| File | Why |
|------|-----|
| `src/bilt/symbolic/_isolation/_isolate.py` | Turns a symbolic equation tree into an isolated sub-tree for a target variable. This is what makes equations "solvable" in any direction. |
| `src/bilt/symbolic/_isolation/_to_ast.py` | Converts an isolated tree into an `ast.FunctionDef` that a backend can compile. |
| `src/bilt/core/_tensors/_tensors.py` | The `Tensor` dataclass and `tensor()` constructor — the concrete data type that flows through the evaluation pipeline. |
| `src/bilt/core/_tensors/_tensor_alignment.py` | The `AlignmentPlan` and `generate_alignment_plan` logic that the backends consume. |
| `src/bilt/core/_system/_error.py` | The sub-system validation errors (already partially visible from the summary, but full contents would clarify the error hierarchy). |

## Suggested order

1. **`_realize_analysis/_realize.py`** — most critical gap; it's the missing link between "we found a plan" and "here's a callable."
2. **`_system/_decorator.py`** — shows the user-facing `@system` API and how `Implementation` is actually constructed.
3. **`_system/_lowering_and_lifting.py`** + **`_evaluation_context.py`** — complete the evaluation pipeline.
4. **`core/__init__.py`** — confirms the public surface.
5. **`_isolation/_isolate.py`** + **`_isolation/_to_ast.py`** — the symbolic-to-executable bridge.

With the two critical files added, the complete runtime path would be:

```
User code:
  instance = MySystem(a=1, b=2)
  result = instance.c

@system decorator (in _decorator.py):
  → generates property `c` that calls _dispatch_evaluation(self, Attribute("c", float))

_dispatch_evaluation (in _dispatch.py):
  → looks up the active Implementation for the instance's Interface
  → calls implementation.evaluate(inputs, output="c", backend=active_backend)

Implementation.evaluate (in _implementation.py):
  → EvaluationContext()
  → domain binding
  → lower_inputs
  → generate_alignment_plan + backend.apply_alignment
  → get_evaluation → analyser.analyse → realize_plan → backend.chain_case
  → backend_callable.evaluate(mangled_inputs)
  → lift_output
  → domain binding of output
```

# Restructuring Recommendations for Bilt

Based on the full codebase, here are the changes I'd make, grouped by the problem they solve.

---

## 1. Eliminate `exec()`-based code generation in `@system`

**Problem:** `_decorator.py` builds `__init__`, property getters, `__eq__`, and `__repr__` as Python source strings and compiles them with `exec()`. This is the single largest maintainability risk in the codebase:

- No type-checker can follow the generated code.
- Debugging a generated property requires reading a string, not a function.
- The `local_namespace` / `global_namespace` juggling is error-prone (e.g., `_create_properties` injects `_dispatch_evaluation` into a local namespace that the generated code closes over).
- The `# noqa: B010` suppressions on `setattr` calls signal that the linter already distrusts this pattern.

**Recommendation:** Replace the string-based generation with direct function construction. Python's `types.FunctionType` and `property` can be built without `exec`:

```python
# Instead of:
body = [f"value = _dispatch_evaluation(self, self.__bilt_system_attributes__['{parameter}'])", "return value"]
_create_method(cls, name=parameter, method=Method.PROPERTY, args=["self"], body=body, ...)

# Do this:
def _make_property(name: str, dispatch_fn: Callable, attributes: dict):
    def getter(self):
        return dispatch_fn(self, attributes[name])
    getter.__qualname__ = f"{cls.__qualname__}.{name}"
    return property(getter)
```

This is a mechanical rewrite of `_create_initialize_method`, `_create_properties`, `_create_eq_method`, and `_create_repr_method`. The `Method` enum and `_create_decorator` / `_create_method` helpers can be deleted entirely. The generated functions become real, inspectable, type-checkable code.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/core/_system/_decorator.py` (rewritten), `src/bilt/core/_system/_dispatch.py` (unchanged, but now called from real functions).

---

## 2. Extract the evaluation pipeline into named stages

**Problem:** `Implementation.evaluate` in `_implementation.py` is a 20-line method that does five distinct things in sequence:

```
bind domains → lower inputs → align tensors → execute → lift output → bind output domain
```

A failure at any stage produces a generic traceback with no indication of which stage failed. The "output already in inputs" fast path is interleaved with the full pipeline.

**Recommendation:** Define a pipeline of stages:

```python
@dataclass
class EvaluationPipeline:
    stages: tuple[Stage, ...]

class Stage(Protocol):
    def run(self, ctx: EvaluationContext, inputs: dict, output: str, backend: Backend) -> object: ...
```

Each stage is a small, independently testable unit. `Implementation.evaluate` becomes:

```python
def evaluate(self, inputs, output, backend):
    with EvaluationContext() as ctx:
        ctx.domains.bind_all(inputs.values())
        if output in inputs:
            result = inputs[output]
        else:
            result = self._pipeline.run(ctx, inputs, output, backend)
        ctx.domains.bind(result)
        return result
```

The pipeline itself is a list of stages: `LoweringStage`, `AlignmentStage`, `ExecutionStage`, `LiftingStage`. Each stage can log, cache, or be skipped independently.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/core/_system/_implementation.py` (simplified), new file `src/bilt/core/_system/_pipeline.py`.

---

## 3. Replace string-based AST construction in `JAXBackend.chain_case`

**Problem:** `_jax/_backend.py` builds a Python function as a string via f-string interpolation, then re-parses it with `ast.parse`. This:

- Breaks if any parameter name is not a valid Python identifier.
- Bypasses the `JAXASTTransformer` for the chained function (only individual transforms get transformed).
- Is impossible to debug without printing the intermediate string.
- Diverges from `PythonBackend.chain_case`, which uses a closure.

**Recommendation:** Build the `ast.FunctionDef` programmatically:

```python
def chain_case(self, case: BackendCase) -> BackendCallable:
    args = [ast.arg(arg=p, annotation=None) for p in case.parameters]
    body = []
    for interim in case.intermediates:
        call_args = [
            ast.Name(id=p if p in case.parameters else f"{p}_", ctx=ast.Load())
            for p in interim.parameters
        ]
        body.append(ast.Assign(
            targets=[ast.Name(id=f"{interim.name}_", ctx=ast.Store())],
            value=ast.Call(func=ast.Name(id=interim.name, ctx=ast.Load()), args=call_args, keywords=[]),
        ))
    body.append(ast.Return(value=ast.Name(id=f"{case.return_name}_", ctx=ast.Load())))
    func_def = ast.FunctionDef(name=case.name, args=ast.arguments(args=args, ...), body=body, ...)
    ast.fix_missing_locations(func_def)
    return self.transpile(func_def)
```

This ensures the `JAXASTTransformer` (which wraps functions in `jax.jit` and converts lists to `jnp.array`) is applied to the chained function. Both backends can then share a common `chain_case` implementation that takes a `BackendCase` and returns a `BackendCallable`, with the only difference being the `transpile` step.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/_backend/_jax/_backend.py`, `src/bilt/_backend/_python/_backend.py`, possibly a new `src/bilt/_backend/_chaining.py` for the shared logic.

---

## 4. Make the isolation algorithm extensible

**Problem:** `_isolation/_rules.py` hard-codes the inverse rules for `ADD`, `MUL`, `NEG`, `INV` in a chain of `if head is ...` checks. Adding a new operator (e.g., `LOG`, `SIN`) requires modifying the core isolation module. The `simplify` function in `_expr_ops.py` has the same structure.

**Recommendation:** Define a registry of isolation rules:

```python
@dataclass
class IsolationRule:
    head: Atom
    def apply(self, lhs: Expr, rhs: Expr, target: Expr) -> tuple[Expr, Expr] | None: ...

_RULES: dict[Atom, IsolationRule] = {
    ADD: AddIsolationRule(),
    MUL: MulIsolationRule(),
    NEG: NegIsolationRule(),
    INV: InvIsolationRule(),
}

def isolate_expr_by_inverse_rules(lhs, rhs, target, relation_head):
    ...
    rule = _RULES.get(lhs.rep.head)
    if rule is None:
        raise UnsupportedIsolationError(...)
    new_lhs, new_rhs = rule.apply(lhs, rhs, target)
    ...
```

This makes the isolation layer open for extension without modifying core code. The same pattern applies to `simplify` in `_expr_ops.py`.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/symbolic/_isolation/_rules.py`, `src/bilt/symbolic/_isolation/_expr_ops.py`, new file `src/bilt/symbolic/_isolation/_registry.py`.

---

## 5. Replace string-based port naming with a structured type

**Problem:** Sub-system ports are represented as dotted strings (`"resistor.v"`) that are then mangled to `"resistor__v"` for backend compilation. This is used throughout:

- `QualifiedPort.dotted` produces the string.
- `mangle_name` replaces `.` with `__`.
- `_NameMangler` in `_realize.py` walks an AST and rewrites every `Name` node.
- `SubSystemConstituent.qualify` / `child_port` do string prefix matching.

The string approach is fragile: a port named `"a.b"` would collide with a sub-system `"a"` with port `"b"`. The `validate_name` check for `MANGLED_SEPARATOR` is a band-aid.

**Recommendation:** Use a structured `Port` type throughout the analysis and realization layers:

```python
@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class Port:
    sub_system: str | None  # None for top-level ports
    name: str

    @property
    def mangled(self) -> str:
        return f"{self.sub_system}__{self.name}" if self.sub_system else self.name
```

The `BipartiteGraph` and `AnalysisPlan` use `Port` objects as node identifiers. Mangling happens only at the boundary where a `BackendCallable` is constructed, not throughout the analysis. This eliminates `_NameMangler` entirely.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/core/_port.py` (extended), `src/bilt/core/_realize_analysis/_realize.py` (simplified), `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_exhaustive/_bipartite.py` (node type change), `src/bilt/core/_sub_system.py` (simplified).

---

## 6. Make `DomainRegistry` immutable after binding

**Problem:** `DomainRegistry` is mutable and uses a `_locked_against_new_dimensions` boolean flag. The object is in a "half-built" state during evaluation. The lock is easy to forget, and the error surfaces at the next `bind` call, not at the point where the lock was needed.

**Recommendation:** Split into two types:

```python
class DomainBinder:
    """Mutable, used during input binding."""
    def bind(self, value: object) -> None: ...
    def bind_all(self, values: Iterable[object]) -> None: ...
    def freeze(self) -> DomainRegistry: ...

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class DomainRegistry:
    """Immutable, used during execution."""
    _bindings: Mapping[Dim, int | CoordinateAxis]
    def size(self, dim: Dim) -> int: ...
    def axis(self, dim: Dim) -> CoordinateAxis: ...
```

The `EvaluationContext` holds a `DomainBinder` during the binding phase and transitions to a `DomainRegistry` before execution. The type system enforces the two-phase lifecycle.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/core/_domain_registry.py` (split), `src/bilt/core/_evaluation_context.py` (updated).

---

## 7. Decouple alignment from the `Backend` protocol

**Problem:** `Backend` (a `Protocol` in `_base_backend.py`) requires `apply_alignment`. But alignment is a tensor concern, not a backend concern. `NDArrayAlignmentMixin` is a workaround that both `PythonBackend` and `JAXBackend` inherit. A future backend that doesn't use NumPy arrays would still be forced to implement or inherit this.

**Recommendation:** Remove `apply_alignment` from the `Backend` protocol. Make it a separate `AlignmentStrategy`:

```python
class AlignmentStrategy(Protocol):
    def apply(self, lowered_inputs: dict[str, object], plan: AlignmentPlan) -> dict[str, object]: ...

class NDArrayAlignmentStrategy:
    def apply(self, lowered_inputs, plan): ...
```

`Implementation.evaluate` (or the pipeline from recommendation 2) applies the alignment strategy before calling the backend. The backend only sees already-aligned inputs.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/_backend/_base_backend.py` (protocol simplified), `src/bilt/_backend/_ndarray_alignment_mixin.py` (renamed to `_alignment.py`), `src/bilt/core/_system/_implementation.py` (pipeline change).

---

## 8. Add a backend/analyser registry

**Problem:** `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` is hard-coded in `Implementation.analyser`. `JAX_BACKEND` and `PYTHON_BACKEND` are module-level singletons with no discovery mechanism. Users cannot plug in a custom analyser or backend without modifying core code.

**Recommendation:** Add a simple registry:

```python
# src/bilt/core/_registry.py
_analysers: dict[str, type[StructuralAnalyser]] = {}
_backends: dict[str, Backend] = {}

def register_analyser(name: str, analyser_type: type[StructuralAnalyser]) -> None: ...
def register_backend(name: str, backend: Backend) -> None: ...
```

`Implementation.analyser` looks up the analyser by name (defaulting to `"exhaustive"`). `Implementation.evaluate` looks up the backend by name (defaulting to `"python"`).

**Files affected:** new file `src/bilt/core/_registry.py`, `src/bilt/core/_system/_implementation.py` (lookup instead of hard-code).

---

## 9. Consolidate the units caching layer

**Problem:** `Unit`, `UnitComposition`, `UnitOrder`, and `Dimension` each use `__new__`-based caching with class-level `_cache` dicts. `Unit.__new__` can return a cached `UnitOrder.unit` directly (a cross-type cache hit). The `Unit` class has three factory paths (`__new__`, `new_named`, `new_absolute`) that each set attributes differently, requiring `# noqa: SLF001` suppressions for private attribute access.

**Recommendation:** Consolidate into a single `UnitRegistry`:

```python
class UnitRegistry:
    _units: dict[tuple[str, UnitComposition, Metadata | None], Unit] = {}
    _names: dict[str, Unit] = {}
    _absolute: dict[Unit, Unit] = {}

    def get_or_create(self, name, relation, metadata=None) -> Unit: ...
    def get_named(self, name, relation, prefix=None) -> Unit: ...
    def get_absolute(self, name, scale, zero=None, metadata=None) -> Unit: ...
```

`Unit` becomes a thin frozen dataclass. The `# noqa: SLF001` suppressions disappear. The caching strategy is testable in isolation.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/units/_units.py` (rewritten), new file `src/bilt/units/_registry.py`.

---

## 10. Make sub-system composition an explicit API

**Problem:** Sub-systems are discovered implicitly: an equation parameter annotated with an `Interface` type is detected by `_sub_system_parameters`, and `SubSystemConstituent` objects are built from `internal_implementations`. The relationship is validated by string-name matching in `_validate_sub_systems`. A sub-system that no equation references is an error (`BiltSubSystemUnreferencedEntryError`), which is surprising.

**Recommendation:** Add an explicit composition API:

```python
@system
class Circuit(Interface):
    v: float = field()
    i: float = field()
    r: float = field()

    @sub_system("resistor", ResistorInterface)
    def resistor(self): ...
```

This makes the parent-child relationship a first-class construct, replaces string-name matching with type-level checks, and allows a sub-system to be composed without requiring an equation to reference it.

**Files affected:** `src/bilt/core/_system/_decorator.py` (new decorator), `src/bilt/core/_system/_implementation.py` (simplified validation), `src/bilt/core/_sub_system.py` (simplified).

---

## Priority and dependency order

| Priority | Change | Depends on | Effort |
|----------|--------|------------|--------|
| 1 | #1 Eliminate `exec()` | None | Medium |
| 2 | #3 JAX `chain_case` | None | Small |
| 3 | #2 Evaluation pipeline | #1 | Medium |
| 4 | #5 Structured `Port` | None | Large |
| 5 | #4 Extensible isolation | None | Small |
| 6 | #6 Immutable `DomainRegistry` | None | Small |
| 7 | #7 Decouple alignment | #2 | Small |
| 8 | #8 Registry | None | Small |
| 9 | #9 Units consolidation | None | Medium |
| 10 | #10 Explicit composition | #5 | Large |

I'd start with #1 and #3 (independent, high impact, no dependencies), then #2 and #7 (which build on each other), then #5 and #10 (which are the largest but most architecturally significant).

The user wants to implement a "scan" or "loop" operation within the bilt framework. Let me think about this carefully.

The use case is:
- A discretized pipe with N elements
- Each element's model takes the previous cell's properties as boundary conditions
- This is a sequential dependency: cell[i] depends on cell[i-1]
- They want this to work in both Python (for loop) and JAX (jax.lax.scan)

Let me think about how this fits into bilt's architecture:

1. **Current bilt model**: Systems are defined as interfaces with equations/transforms. The structural analyser finds a plan through a bipartite graph. The plan is a sequence of independent steps (transforms/equations) that can be chained.

2. **The problem with scan**: A scan is a *sequential* dependency. Cell[i] depends on cell[i-1]. This is fundamentally different from the current model where the bipartite graph finds a DAG of independent computations. A scan introduces a *loop* in the dependency graph.

3. **How bilt currently handles tensors**: The `Tensor` class wraps `np.ndarray` with a `Domain` (ordered dimensions). The `DomainRegistry` binds dimensions to sizes. The alignment system handles broadcasting. But there's no concept of "iterate over a dimension."

4. **The bilt philosophy**: 
   - Equations are symbolic relations that can be solved in any direction
   - Transforms are directional computations
   - The structural analyser finds paths through a bipartite graph
   - Backends compile the plan into a callable
   - The JAX backend wraps functions in `jax.jit`

5. **How to fit scan into this**:

Option A: **Scan as a new model constituent type**
- Add a `Scan` constituent that the bipartite graph can route through
- The scan takes a "body" (a sub-system or transform) and a "dimension" to iterate over
- The structural analyser recognizes the scan and generates a `jax.lax.scan` call for JAX, or a Python for-loop for the Python backend
- This is the most "bilt-native" approach

Option B: **Scan as a transform**
- Define a `@transform` that takes an array and returns an array
- The transform's body is a for-loop (Python) or `jax.lax.scan` (JAX)
- This is simpler but doesn't leverage the structural analyser

Option C: **Scan as a system composition pattern**
- Define a "pipe element" as a sub-system
- The pipe is a system that composes N instances of the pipe element
- The composition is sequential (each element's output feeds the next element's input)
- This is the most "systems of systems" approach

Let me think about which fits bilt's philosophy best...

Bilt's philosophy is:
- **Declarative**: You define relationships, not execution order
- **Directional flexibility**: Equations can be solved in any direction
- **Compositional**: Systems compose from sub-systems
- **Backend-agnostic**: The same model runs on Python or JAX

The scan/loop is fundamentally a *sequential composition* of a sub-system. In bilt terms, it's a "repeated sub-system" where the output of one instance feeds the input of the next.

The most bilt-native approach would be:

1. Define a `PipeElement` interface (the per-cell model)
2. Define a `Pipe` interface that composes N `PipeElement` instances
3. The composition is a "scan" over a dimension
4. The structural analyser recognizes this as a sequential dependency
5. The backend lowers it to `jax.lax.scan` (JAX) or a for-loop (Python)

But there's a tension: the current bipartite graph is a DAG. A scan introduces a *loop* (cell[i] depends on cell[i-1]). The graph would need to be unrolled or the scan would need to be a special node type.

Actually, thinking more carefully:

The scan doesn't need to be a loop in the graph. It can be:
- A single node in the bipartite graph that represents "apply this sub-system N times"
- The node's inputs are: the initial state, the "driver" array (e.g., geometry), and the number of steps
- The node's output is: the final state (or the full trajectory)
- The structural analyser treats it as a single constituent
- The backend lowers it to `jax.lax.scan` or a Python loop

This is cleaner. The scan is a *new constituent type* that the bipartite graph can route through.

```python
class PipeElement(Interface):
```


Actually, let me think about this differently. The key insight is:

1. The **per-element model** is a system (interface + equations/transforms)
2. The **pipe** is a *composition* of that system, repeated N times
3. The repetition is over a dimension (the axial/spatial dimension)
4. The "carry" (state that flows between elements) is the set of properties that are outputs of one element and inputs of the next

In bilt's current architecture:
- The bipartite graph handles DAGs
- A scan is a *loop* that needs to be unrolled or recognized as a special pattern
- The backend needs to lower it to `jax.lax.scan` or a Python loop

The cleanest approach within bilt's philosophy:

**A `Scan` constituent type** that:
- Takes a sub-system (the per-element model)
- Takes a "carry" specification (which ports are carried between iterations)
- Takes a "driver" specification (which ports vary per iteration, e.g., geometry)
- Takes a dimension to iterate over
- The bipartite graph treats it as a single node
- The backend lowers it to `jax.lax.scan` (JAX) or a Python for-loop

The API would be something like:

```python
@system
class PipeElement(Interface):
    p: float = field()
    T: float = field()
    rho: float = field()
    dx: float = field()
    dp: float = field()
    dT: float = field()
    drho: float = field()

@equation
def momentum(p, dp, dx, ...):
    return dp == f(p, dx, ...)

# The pipe is a scan of PipeElement
pipe = scan(
    system=PipeElement,
    carry=("p", "T", "rho"),  # these flow between elements
    drivers=("dx",),           # these vary per element
    over=Dim("axial"),
)
```

Or even more bilt-like, using the existing equation/transform system:

```python
@transform
def pipe_element(p, T, rho, dx) -> tuple[float, float, float]:
    """One element of the pipe."""
    dp = compute_dp(p, dx)
    dT = compute_dT(T, dx)
    drho = compute_drho(rho, dx)
    return p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho

# The scan is a new constituent that the bipartite graph can route through
@scan(pipe_element, over="axial", carry=("p", "T", "rho"))
def pipe(p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx):
    ...
```

Actually, I think the user is asking for a design discussion, not a code change. They want to know *how* to do this within bilt's philosophy. Let me give a thorough architectural answer.

Key considerations:
1. The scan is a sequential dependency - the bipartite graph is a DAG, so we need to handle this
2. The carry state (p, T, rho) flows between iterations
3. The driver (dx, geometry) varies per iteration
4. The backend needs to lower to jax.lax.scan or Python loop
5. The structural analyser needs to understand the scan as a constituent

# Scan / Sequential Composition in Bilt

This is a great question because it exposes a gap in the current architecture: the bipartite graph is a **DAG**, but a scan is a **sequential dependency** (cell *i* depends on cell *i−1*). The design needs to fit this into bilt's declarative, backend-agnostic philosophy without breaking the existing model.

---

## The Core Insight

A scan is **not a loop in the bipartite graph**. It is a **single constituent node** that the backend knows how to lower. The structural analyser never unrolls it. This keeps the graph a DAG and preserves the "find a path, then compile it" separation.

```
Bipartite graph (DAG):

  p_in ──┐
  T_in ──┤
  rho_in ─┤
  dx ─────┤──► [Scan(PipeElement, over=axial)] ──► p_out
  n ──────┤
          │
          └──► T_out
              rho_out
```

The scan node has:
- **Carry inputs**: `p_in`, `T_in`, `rho_in` (scalars, the initial state)
- **Driver inputs**: `dx` (a tensor over the `axial` dimension, varies per step)
- **Carry outputs**: `p_out`, `T_out`, `rho_out` (scalars, the final state)
- **Optional trajectory outputs**: `p_traj`, `T_traj`, `rho_traj` (tensors over `axial`)

The backend lowers this single node to `jax.lax.scan` or a Python `for` loop.

---

## Proposed API

### 1. Define the per-element model as a normal bilt system

```python
@system
class PipeElement(Interface):
    p: float = field()
    T: float = field()
    rho: float = field()
    dx: float = field()
    dp: float = field()
    dT: float = field()
    drho: float = field()

@equation
def momentum(p, dp, dx, rho, ...):
    return dp == f(p, rho, dx)

@equation
def energy(T, dT, dx, ...):
    return dT == g(T, dx)

@equation
def continuity(rho, drho, dx, ...):
    return drho == h(rho, dx)
```

This is a normal bilt system. It can be solved in any direction. The structural analyser already handles it.

### 2. Define the scan as a new constituent type

```python
# New file: src/bilt/core/_scan.py

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class ScanConstituent:
    """A constituent that applies a sub-system repeatedly over a dimension."""

    __bilt_name__: str
    __bilt_parameters__: tuple[str, ...]
    body: Implementation          # the per-element system
    carry: tuple[str, ...]        # ports that flow between iterations
    drivers: tuple[str, ...]      # ports that vary per iteration
    over: Dim                    # the dimension to scan over
    n_steps: str | None          # optional: a parameter that sets the number of steps
```

The `__bilt_parameters__` are the **parent-facing** ports:

```
carry inputs:  p_in, T_in, rho_in
driver inputs: dx
carry outputs: p_out, T_out, rho_out
(optional) trajectory outputs: p_traj, T_traj, rho_traj
```

The bipartite graph treats `ScanConstituent` as a single node, just like `BasicTransform` or `BasicEquation`. The `required_inputs_for_target` function in `_bipartite.py` gets a new case:

```python
case ScanConstituent() as scan:
    if plan_step.output in scan.carry:
        # output is a carry output; needs carry inputs + drivers
        return frozenset(scan.carry_inputs + scan.drivers)
    elif plan_step.output in scan.trajectory_outputs:
        # output is a trajectory; needs carry inputs + drivers
        return frozenset(scan.carry_inputs + scan.drivers)
    else:
        raise BiltSystemAnalysisError(...)
```

### 3. The scan is declared at the pipe level

```python
@system
class Pipe(Interface):
    p_in: float = field()
    T_in: float = field()
    rho_in: float = field()
    p_out: float = field()
    T_out: float = field()
    rho_out: float = field()
    dx: float = field()       # over axial dimension

# The scan constituent wires the per-element model
pipe_scan = ScanConstituent(
    name="pipe",
    body=PipeElement_implementation,
    carry=("p", "T", "rho"),
    drivers=("dx",),
    over=Dim("axial"),
)

# Register it in the pipe's implementation
@system
class Pipe(Interface):
    ...

pipe_impl = Implementation(
    system=Pipe,
    components=[pipe_scan],
)
```

### 4. The user calls it like any other system

```python
pipe = Pipe(
    p_in=101325.0,
    T_in=300.0,
    rho_in=1.2,
    dx=tensor([0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5], over=Dim("axial")),
)

# Solve for the outlet conditions
p_out = pipe.p_out
T_out = pipe.T_out
```

The structural analyser finds the path: `p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx → ScanConstituent → p_out`. The backend compiles it.

---

## Backend Lowering

### JAX backend

The `ScanConstituent` is lowered to `jax.lax.scan`. The per-element model is compiled as a separate function (the "body" of the scan):

```python
# In _realize.py, a new case:
case ScanConstituent() as scan:
    # 1. Compile the per-element body as a BackendCallable
    body_callable = scan.body.get_evaluation(
        EvaluationRequest(output="dp", available=frozenset(scan.carry + scan.drivers)),
        backend,
    )
    # 2. Build a jax.lax.scan call
    #    carry = (p, T, rho)
    #    driver = dx
    #    body(carry, driver) -> (new_carry, trajectory)
    scan_ast = _build_scan_ast(body_callable, scan, backend)
    return BackendCallable(name=..., parameters=..., callable=scan_ast)
```

The generated code looks like:

```python
import jax, jax.numpy as jnp

def pipe(p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx):
    @jax.jit
    def body(carry, dx_i):
        p, T, rho = carry
        dp = momentum(p, rho, dx_i)
        dT = energy(T, dx_i)
        drho = continuity(rho, dx_i)
        new_carry = (p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho)
        return new_carry, (p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho)

    init_carry = (p_in, T_in, rho_in)
    final_carry, trajectory = jax.lax.scan(body, init_carry, dx)
    p_out, T_out, rho_out = final_carry
    return p_out, T_out, rho_out
```

### Python backend

The same `ScanConstituent` is lowered to a `for` loop:

```python
def pipe(p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx):
    p, T, rho = p_in, T_in, rho_in
    for dx_i in dx:
        dp = momentum(p, rho, dx_i)
        dT = energy(T, dx_i)
        drho = continuity(rho, dx_i)
        p, T, rho = p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho
    return p, T, rho
```

The key point: **the same `ScanConstituent` node in the bipartite graph produces different code depending on the backend**. This is exactly how bilt already handles transforms (the JAX backend wraps in `jax.jit`, the Python backend doesn't).

---

## What Changes in the Existing Architecture

| File | Change |
|------|--------|
| `src/bilt/core/_scan.py` | **New.** `ScanConstituent` dataclass, `scan()` constructor. |
| `src/bilt/core/_model_constituent.py` | `BiltModelConstituent` protocol already works (it just needs `__bilt_name__` and `__bilt_parameters__`). No change. |
| `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_exhaustive/_bipartite.py` | Add `ScanConstituent` case to `_add_constituent` and `required_inputs_for_target`. |
| `src/bilt/core/_realize_analysis/_realize.py` | Add `ScanConstituent` case to `_operation_to_callable`. This is where the backend-specific lowering happens. |
| `src/bilt/_backend/_jax/_backend.py` | Add a `_build_scan_ast` helper that generates the `jax.lax.scan` call. |
| `src/bilt/_backend/_python/_backend.py` | Add a `_build_scan_loop` helper that generates the `for` loop. |
| `src/bilt/core/_tensors/_tensors.py` | No change. The `Tensor` class already handles the `dx` array over the `axial` dimension. |
| `src/bilt/core/_domain_registry.py` | No change. The `axial` dimension is bound when `dx` is passed as a `Tensor`. |

---

## Why This Fits Bilt's Philosophy

1. **Declarative**: The user defines the per-element model as equations. The scan is a composition, not an imperative loop. The user never writes a `for` loop.

2. **Directional flexibility**: The per-element model is a normal bilt system. It can be solved in any direction. The scan just applies it sequentially. If the user wants to solve for `p_in` given `p_out`, the structural analyser can route through the scan in reverse (if the per-element model supports it).

3. **Backend-agnostic**: The same `ScanConstituent` node produces `jax.lax.scan` on JAX and a `for` loop on Python. The user's model is identical.

4. **Compositional**: The scan is a constituent, just like a transform or an equation. It can be nested (a scan of a scan), composed with other constituents, and routed through the bipartite graph.

5. **Dimensional**: The `dx` driver is a `Tensor` over the `axial` dimension. The `DomainRegistry` binds the dimension size. The scan's output trajectory is a `Tensor` over the same dimension. This is consistent with how bilt already handles tensor inputs.

---

## Open Design Questions

1. **Trajectory vs. final state**: Should the scan always produce the full trajectory, or only the final carry? The JAX `jax.lax.scan` produces both. The Python loop can produce either. I'd suggest: the scan produces the final carry by default, and trajectory outputs are optional (the user asks for them explicitly).

2. **Variable-length scans**: Should `n_steps` be a parameter (a `field()` on the pipe interface) or inferred from the driver tensor's shape? I'd suggest inferred from the driver tensor's shape, with an optional `n_steps` parameter for cases where the driver is a scalar.

3. **Reverse scans**: Should the scan support a `reverse=True` flag (scan from the outlet to the inlet)? This is useful for back-propagation through the pipe. The JAX backend can use `jax.lax.scan` with `reverse=True`. The Python backend can iterate in reverse.

4. **Nested scans**: A pipe with a cross-sectional scan (radial) inside an axial scan. This is a scan of a scan. The bipartite graph handles this naturally (the inner scan is a constituent of the outer scan's body).

The user is asking how a scan/sequential composition could be integrated into a Dulmage-Mendelsohn (DM) type structural analyser. Let me think about this carefully.

The Dulmage-Mendelsohn decomposition is a graph-theoretic algorithm that decomposes a bipartite graph into:
1. **Trivially determined** variables (have a unique path from inputs)
2. **Underdetermined** variables (multiple paths, need additional constraints)
3. **Overdetermined** variables (no path from inputs, need to be eliminated)
4. **Isolated** variables (no path at all)

The key insight of DM decomposition is that it identifies the **maximum matching** in a bipartite graph, which tells you which equations can be solved for which variables, and which variables are structurally redundant or missing.

Now, the current bilt analyser (`ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser`) uses a DFS-based search through a bipartite graph. It's not a DM analyser. But the user is asking how a scan would fit into a DM-type analyser.

The challenge with a scan in a DM context:

1. **DM decomposition works on a static bipartite graph.** A scan introduces a *sequential dependency* that is not a simple edge in the graph. The scan is a "macro" that expands into N copies of the per-element model, connected in series.

2. **The scan is a single node in the current bipartite graph.** But in a DM decomposition, you'd need to either:
   - Treat it as a single node (opaque) and let the backend handle the sequential dependency
   - Expand it into N copies of the per-element model (unrolled) and let DM work on the expanded graph

3. **The key tension**: DM decomposition is about *structural* solvability (can you find a matching?). A scan's solvability depends on the *per-element* model being solvable, which is a structural property. But the scan itself doesn't add new structural constraints - it just repeats the same constraints N times.

4. **For a DM analyser specifically**:
   - The scan node would appear as a single node in the bipartite graph
   - DM would need to know that the scan node "consumes" its carry inputs and driver inputs, and "produces" its carry outputs
   - The per-element model's structural properties (which variables it can solve for) determine whether the scan is structurally valid
   - If the per-element model is underdetermined, the scan is underdetermined
   - If the per-element model is overdetermined, the scan is overdetermined

5. **The DM decomposition of the scan itself**:
   - The scan has a "carry" structure: each iteration's output feeds the next iteration's input
   - This is a *chain* of dependencies, not a DAG
   - In a DM context, this chain is a *path* through the graph
   - The scan is structurally solvable if and only if the per-element model is structurally solvable for the carry variables

6. **Integration approaches**:
   - **Opaque node**: The scan is a single node in the bipartite graph. DM treats it as a black box. The per-element model's structural properties are pre-computed and used to determine the scan's "effective" structural properties.
   - **Expanded graph**: The scan is unrolled into N copies of the per-element model. DM works on the expanded graph. This is more expensive but more precise.
   - **Hybrid**: The scan is a single node, but DM can "look inside" it to determine its structural properties. The per-element model is analysed separately, and its DM decomposition is used to determine the scan's structural properties.

7. **For the fluid pipe use case**:
   - The per-element model (PipeElement) has equations for momentum, energy, continuity
   - Each equation relates p, T, rho, dx, dp, dT, drho
   - The scan applies this model N times, with carry = (p, T, rho)
   - The structural question is: given p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx, can we compute p_out, T_out, rho_out?
   - This is structurally solvable if the per-element model can solve for (dp, dT, drho) given (p, T, rho, dx)
   - The DM decomposition of the per-element model tells us this

8. **The key insight for DM + scan**:
   - DM decomposition identifies the "core" of the system (the part that is structurally determined)
   - A scan's "core" is the per-element model's core, repeated N times
   - The scan's structural properties are the per-element model's structural properties, "lifted" to the carry variables
   - This is a *structural lifting* operation: the per-element model's DM decomposition determines the scan's DM decomposition

9. **Practical implementation**:
   - The scan node in the bipartite graph has a "structural signature" derived from the per-element model's DM decomposition
   - The scan's "inputs" are the carry inputs + driver inputs
   - The scan's "outputs" are the carry outputs (+ optional trajectory outputs)
   - The scan is structurally solvable if the per-element model can solve for the carry variables given the carry inputs + driver inputs
   - This is a *pre-computed* property of the scan, not something DM needs to discover

10. **For the current bilt architecture**:
    - The `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` uses DFS, not DM
    - A DM analyser would replace the DFS with a maximum matching algorithm
    - The scan would be a single node in the bipartite graph
    - The DM analyser would need to know the scan's "structural signature"
    - The scan's structural signature is derived from the per-element model's DM decomposition

# Scan in a Dulmage-Mendelsohn Structural Analyser

This is the right question to ask, because it forces the design to be precise about *what the scan is structurally*, not just *how it executes*.

---

## The Core Tension

DM decomposition works on a **static bipartite graph**: equations on one side, variables on the other. It finds a **maximum matching** to classify every variable as:

| Class | Meaning |
|-------|---------|
| **Trivially determined** | Has a unique path from a known input |
| **Underdetermined** | No path from inputs; needs an additional constraint |
| **Overdetermined** | Multiple paths; structurally redundant |
| **Isolated** | No path at all |

A scan introduces a **sequential dependency** (cell *i* depends on cell *i−1*) that is not a single edge. The question is: how does this fit into a graph that DM can decompose?

---

## The Key Insight: Structural Lifting

A scan does **not** add new structural constraints. It **repeats** the per-element model's constraints *N* times. The structural question is therefore:

> Can the per-element model solve for the carry variables given the carry inputs and driver inputs?

This is a **pre-computed property** of the per-element model, not something the outer DM decomposition needs to discover. The scan's structural signature is **lifted** from the per-element model's DM decomposition.

Concretely, for the pipe:

```
Per-element model (PipeElement):
  Equations:  momentum, energy, continuity
  Variables:  p, T, rho, dx, dp, dT, drho

  DM decomposition (given p, T, rho, dx as known):
    dp  → trivially determined (from momentum)
    dT  → trivially determined (from energy)
    drho → trivially determined (from continuity)

  Structural signature: {p, T, rho, dx} → {dp, dT, drho}
```

The scan's structural signature is the **carry lifting** of this:

```
Scan (Pipe, over axial, N steps):
  Carry in:  p_in, T_in, rho_in
  Driver:    dx (over axial)
  Carry out: p_out, T_out, rho_out

  Structural signature: {p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx} → {p_out, T_out, rho_out}
  (valid iff per-element model can solve for {dp, dT, drho} given {p, T, rho, dx})
```

The scan is a **single node** in the outer bipartite graph. DM treats it as an opaque relation. Its structural properties are inherited from the per-element model.

---

## How the Bipartite Graph Changes

### Current graph (from `_bipartite.py`)

```
Attributes (left)          Constituents (right)
─────────────────          ────────────────────
p_in ──────────┐
T_in ──────────┤
rho_in ────────┤──► [ScanConstituent] ──► p_out
dx ────────────┤
               │
               └──► T_out
                  rho_out
```

The `ScanConstituent` is a single constituent node. It has edges from its inputs to it, and edges from it to its outputs. This is the same shape as a `BasicTransform` or `BasicEquation`.

### What DM needs from the scan node

The DM algorithm needs to know, for each constituent node:

1. **Which variables it produces** (its outputs)
2. **Which variables it consumes** (its inputs)
3. **Whether it is structurally solvable** for a given output given a given set of available inputs

For a `BasicEquation`, this is: "can I isolate this parameter?" For a `BasicTransform`, this is: "are all its parameters available?" For a `ScanConstituent`, this is: "can the per-element model solve for the carry variables?"

The third point is the critical one. The scan's solvability is **not** a property of the outer graph. It is a property of the **inner** per-element model. The outer DM decomposition must **query** the inner model's DM decomposition.

---

## The Two-Level DM Decomposition

### Level 1: Per-element model

The per-element model (`PipeElement`) is a normal bilt system. It gets its own DM decomposition:

```python
# Pre-computed once, cached on the ScanConstituent
class ScanConstituent:
    ...
    @cached_property
    def structural_signature(self) -> StructuralSignature:
        """The DM decomposition of the per-element model, projected onto carry variables."""
        inner_analyser = self.body.analyser
        # For each carry output, check if it's structurally solvable
        # given the carry inputs + driver inputs
        carry_inputs = frozenset(self.carry_inputs)
        driver_inputs = frozenset(self.drivers)
        available = carry_inputs | driver_inputs

        solvable_outputs = set()
        for carry_var in self.carry:
            required = inner_analyser.required_inputs_for_target(available, carry_var)
            if required is not None and required <= available:
                solvable_outputs.add(carry_var)
            else:
                solvable_outputs.add(None)  # underdetermined

        return StructuralSignature(
            inputs=available,
            outputs=frozenset(solvable_outputs),
            underdetermined=frozenset(self.carry) - solvable_outputs,
        )
```

This is a **one-time cost** at construction time. It is not re-computed during the outer DM decomposition.

### Level 2: Outer system

The outer system's DM decomposition treats the scan as a single node. The scan's structural signature tells the outer DM algorithm:

- **Which outputs the scan can produce** (the carry outputs that are structurally solvable)
- **Which inputs the scan needs** (the carry inputs + driver inputs)
- **Which outputs are underdetermined** (carry outputs that the per-element model cannot solve for)

The outer DM algorithm uses this to classify the scan's outputs in the outer graph:

```
If the scan's structural signature says {p_out, T_out, rho_out} are all solvable:
    → p_out, T_out, rho_out are "trivially determined" in the outer graph
      (assuming p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx are known)

If the scan's structural signature says {rho_out} is underdetermined:
    → rho_out is "underdetermined" in the outer graph
      (the outer system needs an additional constraint, e.g., a boundary condition)
```

---

## The DM Algorithm Itself

The current `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` uses DFS. A DM analyser would replace this with a **maximum matching** algorithm. The standard approach is the **Hopcroft-Karp algorithm** (O(√V · E)) or a simpler **augmenting path** algorithm.

### What changes in the bipartite graph

The bipartite graph in `_bipartite.py` already has the right shape: attributes on one side, constituents on the other. The DM algorithm works on this graph directly. The only change is that `ScanConstituent` nodes carry a `structural_signature` that the DM algorithm consults.

```python
# In _bipartite.py, the _add_constituent method:
case ScanConstituent() as scan:
    # Add the scan as a single constituent node
    plan_step = PlanStep(operation=scan, output=scan.__bilt_name__)
    self.graph.add_node(plan_step, bipartite="constituent")
    self.graph.add_node(scan.__bilt_name__, bipartite="attribute")
    self.graph.add_edge(plan_step, scan.__bilt_name__)

    # Add edges from inputs to the scan
    for param in scan.__bilt_parameters__:
        self.graph.add_node(param, bipartite="attribute")
        self.graph.add_edge(param, plan_step)

    # The scan's structural signature is pre-computed
    # and stored on the node for the DM algorithm to query
    self.graph.nodes[plan_step]["structural_signature"] = scan.structural_signature
```

### The DM decomposition

```python
# New file: src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_dm/_decomposition.py

@dataclass(frozen=True, slots=True)
class DMDecomposition:
    trivially_determined: frozenset[str]
    underdetermined: frozenset[str]
    overdetermined: frozenset[str]
    isolated: frozenset[str]
    matching: dict[str, str]  # variable → constituent that produces it

def dm_decompose(graph: BipartiteGraph) -> DMDecomposition:
    """Decompose the bipartite graph using Dulmage-Mendelsohn."""
    # 1. Find a maximum matching (Hopcroft-Karp or augmenting paths)
    matching = _maximum_matching(graph)

    # 2. Classify variables
    #    - Trivially determined: matched, reachable from a known input
    #    - Underdetermined: unmatched, not reachable from a known input
    #    - Overdetermined: matched, but has an alternative path
    #    - Isolated: no edges at all

    # 3. For ScanConstituent nodes, consult the structural signature
    #    to determine if the scan's outputs are structurally solvable
    ...
```

### How the scan's structural signature feeds into DM

The critical step is in the classification. When the DM algorithm encounters a `ScanConstituent` node:

```python
# In the classification step:
for node in graph.constituent_nodes():
    if isinstance(node.operation, ScanConstituent):
        signature = node.operation.structural_signature
        # The scan's outputs are "trivially determined" iff
        # the per-element model can solve for them
        for output in node.operation.carry_outputs:
            if output in signature.solvable_outputs:
                trivially_determined.add(output)
            else:
                underdetermined.add(output)
    else:
        # Normal DM classification for equations and transforms
        ...
```

This is the **structural lifting** in action: the scan's structural properties are inherited from the per-element model, and the outer DM decomposition uses them to classify the scan's outputs.

---

## What This Means for the Realization Phase

The DM decomposition tells you **which** variables are structurally solvable. The realization phase (in `_realize.py`) then compiles the plan. For a scan, the realization is:

```python
# In _realize.py, a new case:
case ScanConstituent() as scan:
    # 1. The DM decomposition has already verified that the scan is structurally solvable
    # 2. Compile the per-element model as a BackendCallable
    body_callable = scan.body.get_evaluation(
        EvaluationRequest(
            output=scan.carry[-1],  # the last carry variable
            available=frozenset(scan.carry_inputs + scan.drivers),
        ),
        backend,
    )
    # 3. Lower to jax.lax.scan or a Python for loop
    return _lower_scan(body_callable, scan, backend)
```

The DM decomposition and the realization are **decoupled**: DM determines *whether* the scan is solvable, realization determines *how* to compile it. This is the same separation that already exists for equations (DM finds the path, `realize_plan` compiles it).

---

## Summary of Changes

| File | Change |
|------|--------|
| `src/bilt/core/_scan.py` | **New.** `ScanConstituent` with `structural_signature` cached property. |
| `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_dm/_decomposition.py` | **New.** DM decomposition algorithm. |
| `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_dm/_matching.py` | **New.** Maximum matching (Hopcroft-Karp). |
| `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_exhaustive/_bipartite.py` | Add `ScanConstituent` case to `_add_constituent`. Store `structural_signature` on the node. |
| `src/bilt/core/_structural_analysis/_exhaustive/_backend.py` | Replace DFS with DM decomposition. The `analyse` method calls `dm_decompose` instead of `search`. |
| `src/bilt/core/_realize_analysis/_realize.py` | Add `ScanConstituent` case to `_operation_to_callable`. |
| `src/bilt/_backend/_jax/_backend.py` | Add `_lower_scan` that generates `jax.lax.scan`. |
| `src/bilt/_backend/_python/_backend.py` | Add `_lower_scan` that generates a `for` loop. |

---

## The Design Principle

The scan is **structurally transparent** to the outer DM decomposition. It is a single node whose structural properties are **lifted** from the per-element model. The outer DM algorithm never unrolls the scan. It queries the scan's pre-computed structural signature. This keeps the outer graph small (O(1) nodes per scan, not O(N)) and the DM decomposition fast.

The per-element model is analysed **once**, at construction time. Its DM decomposition is cached on the `ScanConstituent`. The outer DM decomposition uses this cache. This is the same pattern as `Implementation.analyser` being a `cached_property`.

Would you like me to draft the `ScanConstituent` and `structural_signature` implementation, or the DM decomposition algorithm?
