Yes. Nested scans fall out naturally from the structural-lifting design, with one caveat that needs to be handled explicitly.

## Why it works

The bipartite graph is a DAG. A nested scan is just a `ScanConstituent` whose `body` (an `Implementation`) contains another `ScanConstituent`. The outer graph has **one node** for the outer scan. The inner scan is a node in the **body's** graph, not the outer graph. No cycle is introduced.

```
Outer graph (Pipe):
  p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx ──► [ScanConstituent: axial] ──► p_out, T_out, rho_out

Body graph (PipeElement, the per-axial-cell model):
  p, T, rho, dx ──► [ScanConstituent: radial] ──► p_mean, T_mean, rho_mean
  p_mean, T_mean, rho_mean ──► [equation: axial_momentum] ──► dp
  ...
```

The outer scan's `structural_signature` is computed by running DM on the body graph. The inner scan is a node in that graph. The inner scan's own `structural_signature` is already cached (computed at its construction time). The outer DM decomposition **queries** the inner signature to classify the inner scan's outputs. This is a recursive query, but it terminates because each level's signature is a `cached_property`.

## The one caveat: recursive signature computation

The outer scan's `structural_signature` must query the inner scan's signature. This is a recursive call:

```python
# In ScanConstituent:
@cached_property
def structural_signature(self) -> StructuralSignature:
    inner_analyser = self.body.analyser
    available = frozenset(self.carry_inputs + self.drivers)

    solvable = set()
    for carry_var in self.carry:
        required = inner_analyser.required_inputs_for_target(available, carry_var)
        if required is not None and required <= available:
            solvable.add(carry_var)

    return StructuralSignature(inputs=available, outputs=frozenset(solvable))
```

When `inner_analyser.required_inputs_for_target` encounters the inner `ScanConstituent`, it queries the inner scan's `structural_signature`, which in turn queries its own body's analyser, and so on. This terminates because:

1. **The graph is a DAG** — no scan's body can contain itself (or a cycle of scans).
2. **Each signature is cached** — the inner scan's signature is computed once, at construction time. The outer scan's signature is computed once, at its construction time. The total cost is O(number of scan nodes), not O(N_outer × N_inner).

The one thing that must be **explicitly checked** is that no scan's body contains itself (directly or transitively). This is a construction-time check:

```python
# In ScanConstituent.__init__:
def __init__(self, name, body, carry, drivers, over, ...):
    ...
    self._check_no_cycle(body, self)   # raises if body contains self transitively
```

This is the same kind of check that `_validate_sub_systems` already does for sub-system name collisions.

## What the generated code looks like

### JAX backend

The outer scan is lowered to `jax.lax.scan`. Its body is the per-element model, which contains the inner scan. The inner scan is also lowered to `jax.lax.scan`. The result is a nested `jax.lax.scan`:

```python
def pipe(p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx):
     @jax.jit
    def outer_body(carry, dx_i):
        p, T, rho = carry
         @jax.jit
        def inner_body(carry_r, dx_r):
            p_r, T_r, rho_r = carry_r
            dp_r = radial_momentum(p_r, rho_r, dx_r)
            dT_r = radial_energy(T_r, dx_r)
            drho_r = radial_continuity(rho_r, dx_r)
            new_carry_r = (p_r + dp_r, T_r + dT_r, rho_r + drho_r)
            return new_carry_r, new_carry_r

        init_r = (p, T, rho)
        final_r, traj_r = jax.lax.scan(inner_body, init_r, dx_radial)
        p_mean, T_mean, rho_mean = jnp.mean(final_r, axis=0)
        dp = axial_momentum(p_mean, rho_mean, dx_i)
        dT = axial_energy(T_mean, dx_i)
        drho = axial_continuity(rho_mean, dx_i)
        new_carry = (p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho)
        return new_carry, new_carry

    init = (p_in, T_in, rho_in)
    final, traj = jax.lax.scan(outer_body, init, dx)
    return final
```

JAX handles nested `jax.lax.scan` natively. The outer scan is vectorised over `axial`, the inner scan is vectorised over `radial`. The total number of per-element evaluations is `N_axial × N_radial`, which is correct.

### Python backend

The same structure, with `for` loops instead of `jax.lax.scan`:

```python
def pipe(p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx):
    p, T, rho = p_in, T_in, rho_in
    for dx_i in dx:
        p_r, T_r, rho_r = p, T, rho
        for dx_r in dx_radial:
            dp_r = radial_momentum(p_r, rho_r, dx_r)
            dT_r = radial_energy(T_r, dx_r)
            drho_r = radial_continuity(rho_r, dx_r)
            p_r, T_r, rho_r = p_r + dp_r, T_r + dT_r, rho_r + drho_r
        p_mean, T_mean, rho_mean = mean(p_r, T_r, rho_r)
        dp = axial_momentum(p_mean, rho_mean, dx_i)
        dT = axial_energy(T_mean, dx_i)
        drho = axial_continuity(rho_mean, dx_i)
        p, T, rho = p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho
    return p, T, rho
```

## What about more complex nesting?

| Case | Works? | Why |
|------|--------|-----|
| Scan of a scan | Yes | Inner scan is a node in the body's graph. Outer signature queries inner signature. |
| Scan whose body has two scans | Yes | Body's DM decomposition handles multiple scan nodes. Each signature is queried independently. |
| Scan whose body has a scan and an equation | Yes | The equation is a normal node. The scan's signature is queried. The equation's solvability is checked normally. |
| Scan whose carry is the output of an inner scan | Yes | The inner scan's output is an input to the outer scan's carry update. The body's DM decomposition classifies it. |
| Reverse scan inside a forward scan | Yes | Direction is a property of the inner scan. The outer scan just queries the inner signature. |
| Scan of a scan of a scan | Yes | Each level's signature is cached. The recursion depth is the nesting depth. |

## The one thing that does not work

A scan whose body **contains itself** (directly or transitively). This is a cycle in the scan nesting, not a cycle in the bipartite graph. It is detected at construction time by the `_check_no_cycle` check. This is the same kind of check that prevents a sub-system from referencing itself.

## Summary

Nested scans work because:

1. **The bipartite graph is a DAG** — nesting does not introduce cycles.
2. **Structural signatures are cached** — the recursion terminates.
3. **The backends handle nesting natively** — `jax.lax.scan` nests, Python `for` loops nest.
4. **The only new check is a construction-time cycle check** — the same pattern as sub-system validation.

The architecture does not need to change to support nesting. The `ScanConstituent` design already supports it. The only addition is the cycle check and the recursive signature computation (which is already implied by the `cached_property` design).

Let me think about what bilt currently supports:
- Equations (symbolic relations, solvable in any direction)
- Transforms (directed computations)
- Sub-systems (composition of systems)
- Scan (proposed, sequential application)
- Tensor broadcasting over dimensions
- Unit/dimensional analysis
- JAX and Python backends

What's missing that would be useful for physical system modeling?

1. **Reduction operations** (sum, mean, min, max over a dimension) - The scan produces trajectories, but you often need to reduce them. E.g., total pressure drop = sum of per-element drops. This is a common operation in pipe modeling.

2. **Conditional / branching** - "If temperature > 100°C, use steam tables; else use ideal gas." This is a piecewise model. In bilt's philosophy, this could be a constituent that selects between two sub-systems based on a condition.

3. **Fixed-point / iterative solvers** - Some physical models require iterative convergence (e.g., solving for a flow rate that satisfies a pressure drop constraint). This is different from scan because it's not sequential - it's a convergence loop.

4. **Event detection / discontinuities** - In pipe flow, you might have a phase change (liquid to vapor) at a certain point. This is a discontinuity in the model.

5. **Optimization** - Finding the pipe diameter that minimizes cost subject to pressure drop constraints. This is a different kind of "solve" than equation isolation.

6. **Time-stepping / ODE integration** - For transient models, you need to integrate over time. This is like a scan but with a specific structure (Euler, RK4, etc.).

7. **Lookup tables / interpolation** - Fluid properties as a function of T and P are often tabulated. You need interpolation.

8. **Boundary conditions** - In a pipe, you might have "p_out = 101325 Pa" as a boundary condition. This is a constraint that the scan must satisfy.

9. **Parallel / independent composition** - Multiple independent sub-systems that don't interact. The bipartite graph already handles this, but there might be a convenience API.

10. **Differentiation / sensitivity** - Computing derivatives of outputs w.r.t. inputs. JAX's autodiff makes this easy, but bilt doesn't expose it.

11. **Constraint satisfaction** - "The total mass flow must equal the inlet mass flow." This is a global constraint on a scan.

12. **Discretization operators** - Finite difference, finite volume, etc. These are transforms that map a field to its derivative.

# Operations Not Yet Implemented in Bilt

Based on the pipe-modelling use case and the broader philosophy of bilt (declarative, directional, backend-agnostic, structurally analysable), here are the operations that would be most useful, ordered by how naturally they fit the existing architecture.

---

## 1. Reduction (sum, mean, min, max over a dimension)

**Why it's needed:** A scan produces a trajectory (a tensor over `axial`). You almost always need to reduce it: total pressure drop, mean temperature, maximum velocity.

**How it fits:** A reduction is a constituent that consumes a tensor over a dimension and produces a scalar. Structurally it is a single node in the bipartite graph, just like a transform. The DM decomposition treats it as a directed edge (tensor → scalar). It is not solvable in reverse (you cannot recover a tensor from its sum), so it is a `BasicTransform`, not a `BasicEquation`.

```python
@transform
def total_pressure_drop(dp: Tensor) -> float:
    return dp.sum()
```

The JAX backend lowers this to `jnp.sum`. The Python backend lowers it to `sum()`. No new constituent type is needed — this is already expressible as a transform. The only gap is that the current transform system doesn't handle `Tensor`-typed parameters in the bipartite graph (the graph tracks scalar attributes, not tensor attributes).

**Architectural change:** Extend `BiltModelConstituent` to declare whether a parameter is a scalar or a tensor over a dimension. The bipartite graph needs to track tensor attributes as distinct from scalar attributes.

---

## 2. Conditional / Piecewise Model

**Why it's needed:** Fluid properties change at phase boundaries. Above 100°C at 1 atm, water is steam. The model switches. In a pipe, you might have a two-phase region.

**How it fits:** A conditional is a constituent that selects between two sub-systems based on a predicate. Structurally it is a node with two "branches" in the bipartite graph. The DM decomposition must handle the fact that only one branch is active at runtime, but both are structurally present.

```python
@conditional(predicate="T > 100.0")
class TwoPhasePipe(Interface):
    p: float = field()
    T: float = field()
    rho: float = field()

    @branch("liquid")
    def liquid_model(self): ...

    @branch("vapor")
    def vapor_model(self): ...
```

**Architectural challenge:** The bipartite graph currently assumes a single path. A conditional introduces a **choice** that is resolved at runtime, not at analysis time. The DM decomposition must classify the conditional's outputs as "conditionally determined" — solvable if the predicate is true, or if the other branch is solvable. This is a new class in the DM decomposition: **conditionally determined**.

**Backend lowering:** JAX uses `jax.lax.cond`. Python uses `if/else`. The same constituent produces different code.

---

## 3. Fixed-Point / Iterative Solver

**Why it's needed:** Some models require convergence. "Find the flow rate that produces a pressure drop of 50 kPa across the pipe." This is a root-finding problem. It is not a scan (not sequential) and not an equation (not algebraically isolable).

**How it fits:** A fixed-point solver is a constituent that wraps a sub-system and iterates until convergence. Structurally it is a single node. The DM decomposition treats it as a directed edge (inputs → output). The convergence is a runtime concern, not a structural one.

```python
@fixed_point(tol=1e-6, max_iter=100)
class PressureDropSolver(Interface):
    p_in: float = field()
    p_out: float = field()
    Q: float = field()
    dx: float = field()
```

**Architectural challenge:** The fixed-point solver is not directionally flexible. You cannot solve for `p_in` given `Q` and `p_out` by "running the solver backwards." It is a directed constituent, like a transform. The DM decomposition must mark it as a directed edge, not a bidirectional relation.

**Backend lowering:** JAX uses `jax.lax.while_loop` or `jax.lax.fori_loop`. Python uses a `while` loop. The convergence check is part of the body.

---

## 4. Lookup / Interpolation

**Why it's needed:** Fluid properties (density, viscosity, specific heat) are tabulated as functions of temperature and pressure. You need to interpolate.

**How it fits:** A lookup is a constituent that maps a set of coordinates to a value from a table. Structurally it is a directed edge (coordinates → value). It is not solvable in reverse (you cannot find the coordinates that produce a given property value without inverting the table).

```python
@lookup(table=water_properties_table, axes=("T", "p"))
class WaterDensity(Interface):
    T: float = field()
    p: float = field()
    rho: float = field()
```

**Architectural challenge:** The lookup table is data, not a model. It must be passed to the implementation at construction time. The bipartite graph treats the lookup as a directed edge. The DM decomposition marks it as non-invertible.

**Backend lowering:** JAX uses `jax.numpy.interp` or a custom scan over the table. Python uses `scipy.interpolate` or a manual search.

---

## 5. Boundary Conditions

**Why it's needed:** A pipe has an inlet and an outlet. The outlet pressure is a boundary condition: `p_out = 101325 Pa`. This is a constraint that the scan must satisfy.

**How it fits:** A boundary condition is a `BasicEquation` with one parameter:

```python
@equation
def outlet_pressure(p_out: float) -> bool:
    return p_out == 101325.0
```

This is already expressible in bilt. The structural analyser handles it: the equation has one parameter, so it is a directed edge (no input → `p_out`). The DM decomposition classifies `p_out` as "trivially determined" by this equation.

**The gap:** The scan's structural signature must account for boundary conditions. If the scan produces `p_out` and a boundary condition also produces `p_out`, the DM decomposition must detect the conflict (overdetermined) or the complementarity (the boundary condition provides the missing input to the scan).

---

## 6. Time-Stepping / ODE Integration

**Why it's needed:** Transient pipe models require integrating over time. This is a scan over a time dimension, but with a specific structure (Euler, RK4, etc.).

**How it fits:** A time-stepper is a `ScanConstituent` with a specific body structure. The body is the per-timestep model (the ODE right-hand side). The scan applies it over the time dimension.

```python
@scan(
    body=PipeTransient,
    carry=("p", "T", "rho"),
    drivers=("dt",),
    over=Dim("time"),
    integrator="rk4",
)
class TransientPipe(Interface):
    p: float = field()
    T: float = field()
    rho: float = field()
```

**Architectural challenge:** The integrator is a parameter of the scan, not a separate constituent. The scan's body is the ODE right-hand side. The integrator wraps the body. This is a composition of a scan and a transform (the integrator). The bipartite graph must represent this as a single node (the scan) with the integrator as a parameter.

---

## 7. Sensitivity / Differentiation

**Why it's needed:** "How does the outlet pressure change if I increase the inlet temperature by 1 K?" This is a derivative of an output w.r.t. an input.

**How it fits:** Differentiation is not a constituent. It is a **transformation** on the entire system. The user asks for the derivative of `p_out` w.r.t. `T_in`. The structural analyser finds the path from `T_in` to `p_out`. The backend differentiates the compiled callable.

```python
# User code:
dp_out_dT_in = pipe.differentiate("p_out", "T_in")
```

**Architectural challenge:** The differentiation is a post-processing step, not a structural one. The bipartite graph is unchanged. The backend (JAX) uses `jax.grad`. The Python backend uses finite differences. The `Implementation` class needs a `differentiate` method that wraps the evaluation.

---

## 8. Constraint / Global Balance

**Why it's needed:** "The total mass flow at the outlet must equal the total mass flow at the inlet." This is a global constraint on a scan.

**How it fits:** A constraint is a `BasicEquation` that relates the outputs of two constituents:

```python
@equation
def mass_balance(m_in: float, m_out: float) -> bool:
    return m_in == m_out
```

This is already expressible. The structural analyser handles it: the equation has two parameters, so it is a bidirectional edge. The DM decomposition classifies `m_out` as "determined by mass_balance given m_in" or vice versa.

**The gap:** The constraint must be added to the same implementation as the scan. The bipartite graph must connect the scan's output to the constraint's input. This is already handled by the existing sub-system composition.

---

## Summary Table

| Operation | Constituent Type | Directional? | Backend Lowering | New Architecture? |
|-----------|-----------------|--------------|------------------|-------------------|
| Reduction | `BasicTransform` | Directed | `jnp.sum`, `sum()` | Tensor attributes in graph |
| Conditional | `ConditionalConstituent` | Branching | `jax.lax.cond`, `if/else` | New DM class |
| Fixed-point | `FixedPointConstituent` | Directed | `jax.lax.while_loop`, `while` | New constituent type |
| Lookup | `LookupConstituent` | Directed | `jnp.interp`, `scipy` | Data parameter |
| Boundary condition | `BasicEquation` | Bidirectional | Already works | No change |
| Time-stepping | `ScanConstituent` + integrator | Directed | `jax.lax.scan`, `for` | Integrator parameter |
| Differentiation | Post-processing | N/A | `jax.grad`, finite diff | `Implementation.differentiate` |
| Global constraint | `BasicEquation` | Bidirectional | Already works | No change |

---

## Priority for the Pipe Use Case

1. **Reduction** — needed immediately (total pressure drop, mean temperature).
2. **Boundary conditions** — already works, but the scan's structural signature must account for them.
3. **Lookup** — needed for realistic fluid properties.
4. **Conditional** — needed for two-phase flow.
5. **Fixed-point** — needed for design optimisation.
6. **Time-stepping** — needed for transient models.
7. **Differentiation** — useful for sensitivity analysis.
8. **Global constraint** — already works.

The first three are the most impactful for the pipe use case. The first two require no new constituent types (reduction is a transform, boundary conditions are equations). The third (lookup) requires a new constituent type but is structurally simple (a directed edge).

For a backward scan (p_out → p_in): the per-element model must be solvable for p given dp, dx, rho. This is the "backward" direction. The scan would iterate from the outlet to the inlet.

def child_route(self, qualified_output: str) -> EvaluationRequest | None:
    child_output = self.child_port(qualified_output)
    available = set(self.interface.__bilt_parameters__) - {child_output}
    required = self.implementation.analyser.required_inputs_for_target(available, child_output)
    if required is None:
        return None
    return EvaluationRequest(output=child_output, available=required)
```

The `SubSystemConstituent` queries the child's analyser for the specific output requested. It doesn't pre-compute a single direction. The scan should work the same way.

The difference is that a scan has a **sequential dependency** that a sub-system doesn't. The scan's carry variables form a chain: p_0 → p_1 → p_2 → ... → p_N. The structural question is: can the per-element model produce the carry update in the required direction?

For a forward scan:
- The per-element model must solve for dp given p, T, rho, dx
- The scan iterates: p_{i+1} = p_i + dp_i
- The structural signature is: {p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx} → {p_out, T_out, rho_out}

For a backward scan:
- The per-element model must solve for p given dp, T, rho, dx (or equivalently, solve for p_{i-1} given p_i)
- The scan iterates backwards: p_{i-1} = p_i - dp_i
- The structural signature is: {p_out, T_out, rho_out, dx} → {p_in, T_in, rho_in}

The key insight: the scan's structural signature is **direction-dependent**. It is not a single pre-computed signature. It is computed on-demand for each requested output.

def required_parent_inputs(self, qualified_output: str) -> frozenset[str] | None:
    route = self.child_route(qualified_output)
    if route is None:
        return None
    return frozenset(self.qualify(port) for port in route.available)
```

This is exactly the pattern the scan should follow. The scan's `required_inputs_for_target` should query the per-element model's analyser for the specific direction needed.

But there's a subtlety: the scan has a **carry** structure. The carry variables are not independent. They form a chain. For a forward scan, the carry chain is p_0 → p_1 → ... → p_N. The scan produces p_N from p_0.
For a backward scan, the carry chain is p_N → p_{N-1} → ... → p_0. The scan produces p_0 from p_N.

The structural question for a backward scan is: can the per-element model solve for p_{i-1} given p_i? This is equivalent to: can the per-element model solve for p given dp (where dp = p_i - p_{i-1})?

This is a directional question about the per-element model. The per-element model's analyser can answer it.

# Scans as Bidirectional Relations

This is the right question, and the answer is: **no, a scan does not have to be directed.** The scan should be a relation node in the bipartite graph, and its structural signature should be computed on-demand for each requested output. This is already the pattern that `SubSystemConstituent` uses.

---

## The Mistake in My Earlier Design

I described the scan's `structural_signature` as a `cached_property` that pre-computes a single direction:

```python
# WRONG: pre-computes one direction
@cached_property
def structural_signature(self) -> StructuralSignature:
    ...
    return StructuralSignature(inputs=carry_inputs, outputs=carry_outputs)
```

This bakes in a forward direction. It cannot answer "can I solve for `p_in` given `p_out`?"

---

## The Correct Pattern: On-Demand Directional Query

Look at what `SubSystemConstituent` already does in `src/bilt/core/_sub_system.py`:

```python
def required_parent_inputs(self, qualified_output: str) -> frozenset[str] | None:
    route = self.child_route(qualified_output)
    if route is None:
        return None
    return frozenset(self.qualify(port) for port in route.available)
```

It does **not** pre-compute a single direction. It queries the child's analyser for the specific output requested. The scan should do the same.

The scan's `required_inputs_for_target` should be:

```python
# In the bipartite graph's required_inputs_for_target:
case ScanConstituent() as scan:
    # The scan is a relation. Query the per-element model for the
    # specific direction needed.
    return scan.required_inputs_for_target(plan_step.output)
```

And on the `ScanConstituent` itself:

```python
def required_inputs_for_target(self, output: str) -> frozenset[str] | None:
    """Query the per-element model for the direction needed to produce `output`."""
    # Determine which "side" of the scan the output is on
    if output in self.carry_outputs:
        # Forward direction: carry_in → carry_out
        # The per-element model must solve for the carry update
        # (dp, dT, drho) given the carry state (p, T, rho) and drivers (dx)
        direction = ScanDirection.FORWARD
    elif output in self.carry_inputs:
        # Backward direction: carry_out → carry_in
        # The per-element model must solve for the carry state
        # (p, T, rho) given the carry update (dp, dT, drho) and drivers (dx)
        direction = ScanDirection.BACKWARD
    else:
        return None

    # Query the per-element model's analyser for this direction
    available = self._available_for_direction(direction)
    target = self._target_for_direction(direction)
    required = self.body.analyser.required_inputs_for_target(available, target)
    if required is None:
        return None

    # Map the per-element model's required inputs back to the scan's parameters
    return self._map_to_scan_parameters(required, direction)
```

---

## What This Means for the Bipartite Graph

The scan is a **relation node**, not a directed edge. This is the same shape as `BasicEquation` and `SubSystemConstituent`:

```
Attributes (left)              Constituents (right)
─────────────────              ────────────────────
p_in ──────────┐
T_in ──────────┤
rho_in ────────┤
dx ────────────┤──► [ScanConstituent] ──► p_out
               │
               └──► T_out
                 rho_out
```

Edges go **from every parameter to the scan node**, and **from the scan node to every parameter**. This is the same pattern as `_add_relation` in `_bipartite.py`:

```python
# Existing code for BasicEquation / SubSystemConstituent:
for param in relation.__bilt_parameters__:
    self.graph.add_node(param, bipartite="attribute")

for param in relation.__bilt_parameters__:
    plan_step = PlanStep(operation=relation, output=param)
    self.graph.add_node(plan_step, bipartite="constituent")
    self.graph.add_edge(plan_step, param)
    for param2 in relation.__bilt_parameters__:
        if param2 != param:
            self.graph.add_edge(param2, plan_step)
```

The scan uses the **exact same code**. No new graph construction logic is needed. The scan is a relation. The bipartite graph already handles relations.

---

## What This Means for the DM Decomposition

The DM decomposition classifies variables based on the graph structure. For a bidirectional scan:

| Requested output | Available inputs | DM classification |
|-----------------|-----------------|-------------------|
| `p_out` | `p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx` | Trivially determined (forward scan) |
| `p_in` | `p_out, T_out, rho_out, dx` | Trivially determined (backward scan) |
| `p_out` | `p_in, T_in, rho_in` | Underdetermined (missing `dx`) |
| `p_out` | `p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx, p_out` | Overdetermined (boundary condition conflict) |
| `dx` | `p_in, p_out, T_in, rho_in` | Trivially determined (if per-element model can solve for `dx`) |

The DM decomposition handles all of these naturally because the scan is a relation node. No new DM class is needed.

---

## What This Means for Realization

The direction is determined at realization time, not at construction time. The `realize_plan` function in `_realize.py` gets a `PlanStep` that says "solve the scan for `p_out`" or "solve the scan for `p_in`". The realization queries the per-element model's analyser for the specific direction and generates the appropriate code:

```python
# In _realize.py:
case ScanConstituent() as scan:
    direction = scan.direction_for_output(plan_step.output)

    # Query the per-element model for this direction
    child_route = scan.child_route_for_direction(direction)
    child_callable = scan.body.get_evaluation(child_route, backend)

    # Lower to the appropriate loop
    if direction is ScanDirection.FORWARD:
        return _lower_forward_scan(child_callable, scan, backend)
    elif direction is ScanDirection.BACKWARD:
        return _lower_backward_scan(child_callable, scan, backend)
```

### Forward scan (JAX)

```python
def pipe_forward(p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx):
    @jax.jit
    def body(carry, dx_i):
        p, T, rho = carry
        dp = momentum(p, rho, dx_i)
        dT = energy(T, dx_i)
        drho = continuity(rho, dx_i)
        return (p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho), (p + dp, T + dT, rho + drho)

    final, traj = jax.lax.scan(body, (p_in, T_in, rho_in), dx)
    return final
```

### Backward scan (JAX)

```python
def pipe_backward(p_out, T_out, rho_out, dx):
    @jax.jit
    def body(carry, dx_i):
        p, T, rho = carry
        # Solve the per-element model in reverse:
        # given p_{i+1}, find p_i
        dp = momentum(p, rho, dx_i)
        p_prev = p - dp
        dT = energy(T, dx_i)
        T_prev = T - dT
        drho = continuity(rho, dx_i)
        rho_prev = rho - drho
        return (p_prev, T_prev, rho_prev), (p_prev, T_prev, rho_prev)

    final, traj = jax.lax.scan(body, (p_out, T_out, rho_out), dx[::-1])
    return final
```

### Python backend

Same structure, `for` loop instead of `jax.lax.scan`. The direction is the only difference.

---

## The Key Architectural Point

The scan is **structurally a relation**, not a directed edge. This is the same as `BasicEquation` and `SubSystemConstituent`. The bipartite graph, the DM decomposition, and the structural analyser all treat it as a relation. The direction is a **realization-time** decision, not a construction-time one.

This means:

1. **No new bipartite graph code.** The scan uses `_add_relation`, the same as equations and sub-systems.
2. **No new DM class.** The scan's outputs are classified by the existing DM algorithm.
3. **No pre-computed signature.** The scan's `required_inputs_for_target` queries the per-element model on-demand, the same as `SubSystemConstituent.required_parent_inputs`.
4. **The direction is a parameter of realization, not of the graph.** The `PlanStep` says "solve the scan for `p_out`" or "solve the scan for `p_in`". The realization generates the appropriate loop.

---

## What This Enables

| Use case | How it works |
|----------|-------------|
| Forward pipe (inlet → outlet) | Scan solved for `p_out` given `p_in, T_in, rho_in, dx` |
| Backward pipe (outlet → inlet) | Scan solved for `p_in` given `p_out, T_out, rho_out, dx` |
| Solve for `dx` given `p_in, p_out` | Scan solved for `dx` (if per-element model supports it) |
| Two-phase pipe with phase boundary | Conditional constituent inside the scan body, direction determined by the conditional |
| Design optimisation (find `dx` for target `p_out`) | Fixed-point constituent wrapping the scan, direction determined by the fixed-point solver |

All of these work because the scan is a relation. The structural analyser finds the path. The realization generates the code. The user never specifies a direction.

---

## The One Constraint

The per-element model must support the requested direction. If the per-element model has:

```python
@equation
def momentum(p, dp, dx, rho):
    return dp == f(p, rho, dx)
```

Then the scan can solve for `dp` given `p, dx, rho` (forward), or for `p` given `dp, dx, rho` (backward), or for `dx` given `p, dp, rho` (if `f` is invertible). The structural analyser queries the per-element model's analyser for each direction. If the per-element model cannot solve for the requested variable, the scan's `required_inputs_for_target` returns `None`, and the structural analyser routes around the scan (or reports it as underdetermined).

This is the same constraint that applies to any `BasicEquation`: the equation must be isolable for the requested parameter. The scan inherits this constraint from its body.

---

## Summary

The scan is a **relation**, not a directed edge. It uses the same bipartite graph construction, the same DM decomposition, and the same on-demand directional query as `SubSystemConstituent`. The direction is a realization-time decision. The per-element model's analyser is queried for the specific direction needed. No new structural analysis code is needed. The only new code is in the realization phase (generating the loop) and the backend lowering (generating `jax.lax.scan` or a `for` loop).

Would you like me to draft the `ScanConstituent` implementation with this bidirectional design?

- **`_python/_backend.py`** – `PythonBackend`: executes in-process by rewriting the AST.
- **`_jax/_backend.py`** – `JAXBackend`: transpiles to JAX via a `JAXASTTransformer`.
- **`_in_process_python_mixin.py`** – Shared logic for AST rewrite → compile → execute.

# Functional Programming

Functional programming (FP) is a programming paradigm that treats computation as the evaluation of mathematical functions and avoids changing state and mutable data.

## Core Principles

- **Immutability** – Data is not modified in place. Instead, new values are produced from old ones.
- **Pure functions** – A function's output depends *only* on its inputs, with no side effects (no I/O, no mutation, no global state access). Same input → same output, always.
- **First-class functions** – Functions are values: they can be passed as arguments, returned from other functions, and stored in data structures.
- **Higher-order functions** – Functions that take or return other functions (e.g., `map`, `filter`, `reduce`).
- **Declarative style** – You describe *what* to compute rather than *how* (step-by-step mutation).

## Key Concepts

| Concept | Idea |
|---|---|
| **Recursion** | Replaces loops; a function calls itself on a smaller sub-problem. |
| **Currying** | A function of multiple arguments is transformed into a sequence of single-argument functions. |
| **Partial application** | Fixing some arguments to produce a new, simpler function. |
| **Composition** | Chaining functions: `(f ∘ g)(x) = f(g(x))`. |
| **Monads** | A structure for sequencing computations that carry context (I/O, state, errors) while preserving a pure-looking interface. |
| **Functors / Applicatives** | Abstractions for mapping a function over a wrapped value (e.g., `Option`, `List`, `Future`). |
| **Pattern matching** | Decomposing data structures by shape rather than by type. |
| **Algebraic data types** | Data built from sums (variants/union types) and products (tuples/structs). |

## Languages

- **Purely functional:** Haskell, ML (OCaml, F#), Elm, PureScript
- **Multi-paradigm with strong FP support:** Scala, Clojure, Elixir, Rust (partial), Python, JavaScript/TypeScript, C#

## Benefits

- **Predictability** – Pure functions are easy to reason about, test, and parallelise.
- **Referential transparency** – An expression can be replaced by its value without changing program behaviour.
- **Concurrency friendliness** – No shared mutable state means fewer race conditions.
- **Composability** – Small pure functions combine into larger ones cleanly.

## Trade-offs / Criticisms

- **Learning curve** – Thinking in terms of transformations rather than step-by-step mutation takes practice.
- **Performance** – Immutability can create more allocations; some languages mitigate this with structural sharing or compiler optimisations (e.g., tail-call elimination).
- **I/O** – Real-world programs need side effects; FP languages handle this via monadic effects or effect systems, which can feel abstract.
- **Verbosity** – In some languages, FP-style code is more verbose than imperative equivalents.

## Connection to Your Repo

The `bilt` framework already leans on several FP ideas:

- **Immutable, hash-consed `Tree` and `Atom`** objects (no mutation allowed).
- **Pure transforms** – `@transform` wraps a function whose output depends only on its inputs.
- **Higher-order composition** – The structural analyser composes transforms into a plan; backends transpile that plan.
- **Declarative system definitions** – Users declare *what* relationships hold (equations, fields) rather than *how* to solve them.

If you'd like a deeper dive into any sub-topic (monads, category theory foundations, FP in a specific language, etc.), just ask.

# Aligning `bilt` More Closely with Functional Principles

The framework already has strong FP foundations — hash-consed immutable `Tree`/`Atom`, frozen dataclasses for `Domain`/`Dim`, pure `@transform` wrappers, and a declarative system-definition style. But there are several seams where the design leans on mutable state or imperative mechanisms. Here are the main areas, roughly ordered from "small, high-value" to "large, architectural."

---

## 1. Make `DomainRegistry` Immutable / Copy-on-Write

**Current:** `DomainRegistry` has `bind()`, `bind_value()`, `bind_axis()`, `lock_against_new_dimensions()` — all mutating methods on a single shared instance.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Make `DomainRegistry` a frozen dataclass (or use `__slots__` + `__new__` like `Tree`).
- Each `bind` call returns a **new** `DomainRegistry` with the additional binding.
- `lock_against_new_dimensions` becomes a flag on the frozen struct; attempting to bind after locking raises.
- The evaluation pipeline threads the registry through as an explicit parameter rather than a shared mutable object.

**Trade-off:** More allocations, but the registry is small (a few dimension bindings per evaluation), so the cost is negligible. The big win is that two concurrent evaluations can't accidentally share or corrupt a registry.

---

## 2. Replace the `EvaluationContext` with an Explicit Threaded State

**Current:** `EvaluationContext` is a `@typing.final` mutable object accessed via a module-level `current_evaluation()` — effectively a global.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Make `EvaluationContext` a frozen dataclass.
- Pass it explicitly through the evaluation pipeline: `evaluate(ctx, system, target, inputs) → result`.
- If you want to avoid threading it through every call, use a **Reader monad** pattern or a lightweight dependency-injection container that is itself immutable.
- Remove the module-level global; the "current" context is whatever the caller passes in.

**Trade-off:** Slightly more verbose call signatures, but eliminates a class of bugs where a nested or concurrent evaluation clobbers the outer one. The existing test `test_failed_evaluation_does_not_contaminate_next_evaluation` would become structurally guaranteed rather than a runtime check.

---

## 3. Replace Backend Activation with Explicit Passing

**Current:** `ActivatableBackendMixin` uses `__enter__`/`__exit__` to set a global "active backend." This is a side effect that mutates module-level state.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Drop the context-manager activation entirely.
- `realize_plan(plan, backend)` already takes the backend explicitly — extend that pattern so the entire evaluation entry point takes a `backend` parameter.
- If you want a "default backend" for convenience, make it a parameter with a default value, not a global.

**Trade-off:** Users lose the `with JAXBackend():` sugar, but gain referential transparency. You could keep the context manager as a thin syntactic wrapper that just sets a local variable, not a global.

---

## 4. Make the Structural Analyser Pure

**Current:** `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` builds a mutable `nx.DiGraph` and the `BipartiteGraph.search` method mutates a `visited` set.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Build the graph once as an immutable structure (a frozen adjacency map, or a `frozenset`-based representation).
- `search` takes `visited: frozenset[Node]` and returns a new `frozenset` at each step, or uses a local `set` that is never shared.
- `remove_failed_plan_step` returns a new graph rather than mutating in place.
- The analyser itself becomes a pure function: `analyse(constituents, available, target) → AnalysisPlan | None`.

**Trade-off:** NetworkX's `DiGraph` is mutable by design. You'd either wrap it in a frozen container or switch to a pure-Python adjacency representation. For the graph sizes involved (dozens of nodes), the latter is fine.

---

## 5. Represent Errors as Values, Not Exceptions

**Current:** The codebase has a hierarchy of exceptions (`BiltCoreError`, `BiltBackendError`, `BiltSystemAnalysisError`, `IsolationError`, etc.) that are raised and caught.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Introduce a `Result[T, E]` (or `Either`) type: `Success(value)` or `Failure(error)`.
- `realize_plan` returns `Result[BackendCase, RealizationFailure]` — it already does this partially (`BackendCase | RealizationFailure`).
- Extend the pattern: `analyse(...)` returns `Result[AnalysisPlan, BiltSystemAnalysisError]`.
- The evaluation pipeline becomes a chain of `Result`-returning functions composed with a `bind`/`and_then` operator.
- Exceptions remain as the *outermost* boundary (e.g., the top-level `evaluate` call raises if the final result is a `Failure`), but internal control flow is value-based.

**Trade-off:** More verbose at each step, but you get composable error handling, no stack-trace noise for expected failures, and the ability to recover from intermediate failures without unwinding the stack.

---

## 6. Replace the `@system` Metaclass with a Pure Builder

**Current:** `Interface` is a metaclass that mutates the class namespace during `__new__` — creating properties, methods, decorators, and disabling `__hash__`. This is inherently imperative and hard to reason about.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Keep the `@system` decorator as a user-facing sugar, but internally have it call a **pure function**:
  ```
  build_system(name, fields, equations, implementations) → SystemSpec
  ```
- `SystemSpec` is a frozen dataclass describing the system.
- The "class" the user sees is a thin wrapper around a `SystemSpec`, not a metaclass-mutated type.
- This makes it possible to inspect, serialise, or transform a system definition without executing metaclass logic.

**Trade-off:** This is the largest change. It would break the `class Demo(metaclass=Interface)` syntax unless you keep a compatibility shim. The payoff is that system definitions become data, which is easier to test, serialise, and reason about.

---

## 7. Make the AST Transformation Pipeline Pure

**Current:** `InProcessPythonMixin.transpile` rewrites an `ast.FunctionDef` and then `compile()`s and `exec()`s it. The JAX backend's `JAXASTTransformer` is an `ast.NodeTransformer` (which mutates the tree in place).

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Use `ast.copy` or a structural-copy approach so the transformer produces a **new** tree rather than mutating the input.
- The transformation function is then pure: `transform(tree) → new_tree`.
- The `compile`/`exec` step is the one unavoidable impure boundary (it runs code), but it's isolated and explicit.

**Trade-off:** Minor performance cost from copying ASTs. The benefit is that the original function definition is never mutated, which matters if the same `@transform` is used in multiple evaluations.

---

## 8. Make Caching Explicit and Pure

**Current:** The test `test_repeated_evaluation_reuses_one_cache_entry` implies a module-level or instance-level cache that is mutated on each call.

**FP-aligned alternative:**
- Represent the cache as an immutable map: `dict[frozenset, Result]` (or a persistent data structure).
- Each evaluation returns `(result, new_cache)`.
- The top-level `evaluate` function threads the cache through, or you use a `State` monad to hide the threading.
- Alternatively, use `functools.lru_cache` on a pure function, which is the standard Python idiom and is "pure enough" for most purposes.

**Trade-off:** Explicit threading is verbose; `lru_cache` is pragmatic. The key principle is that the cache is a *value* that flows through the computation, not a hidden global.

---

## Summary: A Suggested Priority Order

| Priority | Change | Effort | Impact |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Make `DomainRegistry` immutable | Low | High — eliminates a shared-mutable-state bug class |
| 2 | Thread `EvaluationContext` explicitly | Low–Med | High — removes a global |
| 3 | Drop backend context-manager activation | Low | Med — removes another global |
| 4 | Make the analyser pure | Med | Med — cleaner, testable in isolation |
| 5 | `Result` types for expected errors | Med | Med — composable error handling |
| 6 | Pure AST transformation | Low | Low–Med — hygiene |
| 7 | Explicit caching | Low | Low — mostly a principle |
| 8 | Replace metaclass with pure builder | High | High — but risky, do last |

The first three are small, localised changes that remove the most egregious impurities (mutable globals) without touching the public API much. The metaclass replacement is the most ambitious and would change the user-facing syntax, so it's a longer-term goal.

Would you like me to sketch a concrete implementation for any of these?

Good question. I think I was importing a Haskell reflex that doesn't translate well to Python, and I should have been more careful.

## Why I Suggested It (and Why It's Weak in Python)

In a language like Haskell, threading is the *only* option — there's no mutable state, no globals, no context managers. You pass the state through every function, and the type system enforces it.

In Python, that same approach has real costs:

- **Verbosity.** Every call signature grows by one parameter. In a deep pipeline like `evaluate → analyse → realize → transpile → evaluate`, that's a lot of noise.
- **No enforcement.** Python won't stop you from forgetting to pass it, or from accidentally using a stale copy. The "discipline" is purely social.
- **Fights Python idioms.** Python's ecosystem is built around context managers, dependency injection, and `contextvars`. Threading feels alien and makes the code harder for a Python reader to follow.
- **It's not actually more "pure."** A frozen `EvaluationContext` passed by reference is already safe — nobody can mutate it. The *safety* comes from the immutability, not from the threading. Threading just makes the dependency *visible*, which is a readability choice, not a correctness one.

## What Would Actually Work Better in Python

**1. Frozen object, passed by reference, not threaded through everything.**

Since Python objects are references, you can pass a frozen `EvaluationContext` to the top-level `evaluate()` call and let it flow through a small number of layers. You don't need to thread it through *every* helper. The key property is:

- It's frozen (nobody mutates it).
- It's created once per evaluation.
- It's not a module-level global.

That gives you referential transparency without the syntactic cost.

**2. `contextvars` for ambient state.**

Python 3.7+ has `contextvars.ContextVar`, which is the language-native answer to "I need ambient state that is structured and concurrency-safe." It's not a global — it's scoped to the current task/coroutine. If the framework ever goes async, this is the right tool. It's less "pure" than threading, but it's *Python's* way of expressing the same idea.

**3. Dependency injection at the boundary.**

Pass the context (or the backend, or the registry) into the top-level entry point. Let internal helpers access it through a small, well-defined interface. This is what most mature Python frameworks do (FastAPI, Click, etc.). It's not "pure FP," but it's *structured* and *testable*, which is the actual goal.

## The Deeper Point

The goal of the FP alignment isn't "make Python look like Haskell." It's:

- **No hidden mutable globals** — the current `current_evaluation()` and backend activation are the real problems.
- **Referential transparency** — a frozen object passed by reference achieves this.
- **Composability** — functions that take their dependencies as parameters are easier to test and combine.

Threading is one way to achieve those goals. In Python, a frozen object + a small number of injection points achieves the same goals with less friction. I should have said that instead of defaulting to the Haskell pattern.

Does that distinction make sense, or is there a specific part of the pipeline where you're seeing the threading cost bite?

The output is empty (just terminal control characters, no diff content). That means:

**`HEAD` and `main` point to the same commit.** There are no changes to discuss.

A few possibilities:

1. **You're on `main` itself.** `HEAD` is just a pointer to `main`, so `main..HEAD` is an empty range.
2. **You're on a branch that was just created from `main`** and no commits have been made yet.
3. **You merged `main` into your branch** (or fast-forwarded), so they're at the same commit.

You can confirm with:

```bash
git log --oneline main..HEAD
```

If that also prints nothing, the two refs are identical.

If you were expecting to see changes, you might want to check:

```bash
git status          # uncommitted work?
git log --oneline -5  # where is HEAD?
git branch -vv      # what does main point to?
```

# A Useful `agents.md` for `bilt`

Here's a draft. It's structured to get an agent productive quickly without reading every file, while flagging the non-obvious architectural decisions that trip up newcomers.

---

```markdown
# bilt — Agent Context

## What this is

`bilt` is a symbolic computation and physics-modelling framework. Users declare
systems (fields, equations, transforms) and the framework solves for any
requested output by searching a bipartite dependency graph, then transpiling
the resulting plan to a chosen backend (Python, JAX, …).

## Repository layout

    src/bilt/
      symbolic/       Immutable Tree/Atom layer. Hash-consed. No mutation.
      core/           System definition, structural analysis, realisation,
                      domain registry, evaluation context, tensors, ports.
      _backend/       Backend protocol + implementations (Python, JAX).
      units/          Dimensional-analysis unit system (Unit, Quantity, …).
    packages/examples/  Worked examples (heat-exchanger geometry, etc.).
    tests/            pytest suite, parametrised over backends.
    rust/bilt_native/ PyO3 stub (early-stage native extension).

## Key invariants (do not break)

- **Tree and Atom are immutable and hash-consed.** `__setattr__` and
  `__delattr__` raise. Equality is identity-based. Symbolic equality lives
  inside a Tree as an EQUALS atom, not in `Atom.__eq__`.
- **Domain / Dim / CoordinateAxis are frozen dataclasses.**
- **The structural analyser is a search, not a solver.** It finds a *plan*
  (ordered list of PlanSteps). Realisation turns the plan into a
  BackendCase. Do not conflate the two.
- **Backends are activated via context manager** (`with JAXBackend():`).
  This sets a module-level "current backend." It is a known impurity;
  see "Gotchas" below.
- **EvaluationContext is a module-level global** accessed via
  `current_evaluation()`. Same caveat.

## How to run things

    # Tests (parametrised over PythonBackend and JAXBackend)
    pytest tests/

    # A single test
    pytest tests/core/test_system.py::test_evaluation_with_single_implementation

    # Lint / type-check (confirm the exact commands in pyproject.toml)
    ruff check src/
    mypy src/bilt/

## Common tasks

### Add a new transform

    @transform
    def my_fn(a: float, b: float) -> float:
        return a + b

    # Register it in a system implementation:
    @system
    class MySystem(metaclass=Interface):
        a: float = field()
        b: float = field()
        c: float = field()

    MySystem.implement(my_fn)   # or however the registration API works

### Add a new backend

1. Subclass `InProcessPythonMixin` (or write from scratch).
2. Implement `transpile(ast.FunctionDef) -> BackendCallable`.
3. Implement `chain_case(BackendCase) -> BackendCallable`.
4. Mix in `ActivatableBackendMixin` for context-manager activation.
5. Mix in `NDArrayAlignmentMixin` if the backend handles tensors.
6. Add a test in `tests/core/` parametrised alongside the existing backends.

### Add a new example

    packages/examples/src/examples/<domain>/<subsystem>/_module.py

Follow the naming convention: leading underscore, snake_case.

## Error hierarchy

    BiltCoreError
      ├── EquationReturnError
      ├── BiltNameError
      ├── BiltSystemDefinitionError
      │     └── BiltSubSystemMissingImplementationError
      └── BiltSystemAnalysisError

    BiltBackendError
      ├── UnsupportedNodeError
      └── CompilationError

    BiltSymbolicError
      ├── AtomError → AtomMutationError
      └── TreeError → TreeMutationError

    IsolationError
      ├── TargetNotFoundError
      ├── TargetOnBothSidesError
      └── UnsupportedIsolationError

    DimensionalityError / UnitError / MetadataError  (units layer)

Raise the most specific subclass. Do not raise bare `Exception`.

## Gotchas

- **The `@system` metaclass (`Interface`) mutates the class namespace in
  `__new__`.** It creates properties, an `__init__`, `__eq__`, `__repr__`,
  and disables `__hash__`. If you add a new class-level attribute, check
  whether the metaclass needs to know about it.
- **`current_evaluation()` is a global.** Nested evaluations can clobber
  each other. The test `test_failed_evaluation_does_not_contaminate_next_
  evaluation` guards this.
- **Backend activation is also a global.** `with JAXBackend():` sets the
  active backend for the duration of the block. Do not nest backend
  context managers without understanding the interaction.
- **`DomainRegistry` is mutable.** `bind()` / `bind_axis()` mutate in
  place. A fresh registry is created per attribute evaluation, but within
  one evaluation the same instance is shared.
- **The bipartite graph uses NetworkX `DiGraph` (mutable).** The
  `ExhaustiveStructuralAnalyser` builds it once and searches it. Do not
  mutate the graph after construction.
- **AST transformation in `InProcessPythonMixin` compiles and execs
  code.** This is the one unavoidable impure boundary. Keep it isolated.

## Conventions

- **Naming:** private modules use a leading underscore (`_tree.py`,
  `_backend.py`). Public API is re-exported from package `__init__`.
- **Type hints:** full annotations everywhere. `typing.Self` for
  `__enter__`/`__exit__`. `@typing.final` on classes that should not be
  subclassed.
- **Dataclasses:** `frozen=True, slots=True` for value types.
  `@dataclasses.dataclass` (mutable) only for pipeline state
  (`BackendCallable`, `BackendCase`, `PlanStep`, `AnalysisPlan`).
- **Tests:** parametrize over `(PythonBackend(), JAXBackend())` for any
  test that exercises the evaluation pipeline.
- **Docstrings:** one-line summary on every public class/function.
  Expand with a paragraph only when the "why" is non-obvious.

## What I don't know yet (fill in)

- [ ] Exact `pyproject.toml` commands for lint, type-check, build.
- [ ] How `Implementation.evaluate` dispatches to the backend in detail.
- [ ] The full `@system` / `@transform` / `@equation` registration API.
- [ ] Whether the Rust native module is wired into the build yet.
- [ ] CI configuration and what it runs.
- [ ] Any performance-sensitive paths that should not be refactored
      casually.
```

---

## Why this shape

| Section | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **What this is** | One-paragraph orientation. An agent shouldn't have to infer the domain. |
| **Repository layout** | A map, not a file listing. Points to *layers*, not every module. |
| **Key invariants** | The things that are easy to break and hard to notice. This is the highest-value section. |
| **How to run things** | Saves the agent from guessing. |
| **Common tasks** | Recipes for the 3–4 things an agent will actually be asked to do. |
| **Error hierarchy** | Prevents the agent from raising `Exception` or picking the wrong subclass. |
| **Gotchas** | The non-obvious mutable-state and metaclass interactions. Without this, an agent will "fix" something and break it. |
| **Conventions** | Keeps generated code consistent with the existing style. |
| **What I don't know yet** | Honest gaps. The user fills these in as the project evolves. |

The "What I don't know yet" section is important: it tells the agent *and* the next human reader where the context is incomplete, so they don't hallucinate an answer.

Would you like me to adjust the tone, add/remove sections, or tailor it for a specific agent tool (Cursor, Copilot, Aider, etc.)?

# Critique of the Proposed `agents.md`

## What's strong

- **The "Gotchas" section is the highest-value part.** It encodes the non-obvious mutable-state interactions that an agent will inevitably trip over. Without it, an agent will "clean up" the `DomainRegistry` or the backend activation and break things.
- **"Key invariants" is well-scoped.** It names the things that are easy to break and hard to notice, without being a full spec.
- **"What I don't know yet" is honest.** It prevents the agent from hallucinating answers to questions it can't answer from the file summaries.
- **The error hierarchy** saves the agent from raising bare `Exception` or picking the wrong subclass, which is a common failure mode.

## What's weak

**1. It's too long for what it is.**

An `agents.md` competes with the actual conversation for context window. Every line that doesn't change the agent's behaviour is a line that could be spent on the task. I'd cut or compress:

- The "Common tasks" recipes are 6-step procedures that will go stale the moment the API shifts. Replace with a pointer: *"See `tests/core/test_system.py` for the canonical usage pattern."*
- The "Conventions" section is mostly generic Python advice. What's *specific* to `bilt`? The leading-underscore module naming, the `@typing.final` on `Tree`/`Atom`, the `frozen=True, slots=True` on value types — those are the non-obvious ones. Drop the rest.
- The "Why this shape" table at the end is for the human reader, not the agent. Remove it from the file.

**2. The "Add a new backend" recipe is speculative.**

I inferred those 6 steps from partial file summaries. If the actual API differs (e.g., `chain_case` isn't required, or `NDArrayAlignmentMixin` is optional), the agent will follow a wrong recipe. Better to say: *"Subclass `Backend`. See `PythonBackend` and `JAXBackend` as reference implementations."* and let the agent read the actual code.

**3. The pipeline is not explained.**

The single most useful thing an agent needs is the **data flow**:

```
@equation / @transform
  → symbolic Tree (hash-consed)
  → isolation (solve for target)
  → ast.FunctionDef
  → backend.transpile()
  → BackendCallable
  → evaluate(inputs)
```

Without this, an agent has to reverse-engineer the pipeline from scattered files. One diagram or one paragraph would save a lot of exploration.

**4. "What I don't know yet" creates a false boundary.**

Listing 5 unknowns implies those are *the* unknowns. An agent might treat the document as authoritative and not question things that aren't listed. A better framing: *"This document is a starting point. If you're unsure about anything not covered here, read the source. Do not assume the absence of a topic means it doesn't exist."*

**5. It doesn't mention the symbolic → core boundary clearly.**

The `symbolic/` layer (Tree, Atom, Expr, isolation) and the `core/` layer (system, analysis, realisation, evaluation) are two distinct concerns. An agent needs to know: *symbolic is the representation, core is the machinery that operates on it.* The current draft lists them as siblings in the layout but doesn't explain the relationship.

**6. Testing strategy is under-described.**

"Parametrize over backends" is one line. But the test suite also covers symbolic isolation, unit containers, tensor broadcasting, and the exhaustive analyser in isolation. An agent adding a feature needs to know *where* to add tests, not just *how* to parametrize.

## What I'd change

- **Cut the document roughly in half.** Keep: What this is, layout, key invariants, gotchas, error hierarchy, the pipeline diagram. Drop: common-task recipes, generic conventions, the "why this shape" table.
- **Add the pipeline diagram.** One paragraph or a code block.
- **Replace speculative recipes with pointers to reference implementations.**
- **Reframe "What I don't know yet" as a disclaimer, not a checklist.**
- **Add a "where to add tests" note** pointing at the relevant `tests/` subdirectory per layer.

## The deeper question

An `agents.md` is a trade-off between *completeness* and *staleness*. The more specific it is, the more useful it is today, and the more likely it is to be wrong next week. I'd bias toward **pointers and invariants** (which stay true) over **recipes and procedures** (which rot). The agent should be told *what not to break* and *where to look*, not *what to do step by step*.

#### # The Zen of Bilt  
####   
#### 1. **Systems define the model; implementations provide its relationships. Execution is derived.**  
####    Systems define meaningful boundaries and values. Implementations supply equations, transforms and subsystems. Bilt determines how to evaluate them.  
####   
#### 2. **Describe relationships, not procedures.**  
####    State what is true between values. Supply what is known, request what is wanted, and let Bilt determine the route between them.  
####   
#### 3. **Equations express relationships; transforms express direction.**  
####    Use equations for genuinely acausal relationships and transforms where direction is intrinsic to the computation.  
####   
#### 4. **Keep the structure close to the engineering.**  
####    Systems, values and equations should reflect the concepts and mathematics of the problem rather than computational convenience.  
####   
#### 5. **Compose systems rather than building pipelines.**  
####    Build larger models from meaningful subsystems. Connections express shared quantities, not the movement of data from one computation to another.  
####   
#### 6. **Values carry meaning.**  
####    A value's identity, units and domain describe what it represents; they are not merely metadata around an array.  
####   
#### 7. **Implementations should be replaceable.**  
####    Different relationships, numerical methods, backends or levels of fidelity should be able to realise the same system.  
####   
#### 8. **Prefer explicit structure to hidden behaviour.**  
####    Avoid hidden state, accidental broadcasting and implementation tricks that obscure the model or make it harder to analyse.  
####   
#### 9. **Execution should follow transparently from the model.**  
####    It should be possible to inspect why Bilt selected a particular set of relationships and how those relationships produced the requested output.  
####   
#### 10. **Let the modeller provide meaning; let Bilt handle mechanics.**  
####     Modellers define systems, relationships and assumptions. Bilt handles structural analysis, equation isolation, broadcasting, lowering and execution.

This is a strong set of principles. A few thoughts:

**What works well:**

- **Principle 1** is the clearest and most distinctive. "Execution is derived" is a sharp one-liner that immediately differentiates Bilt from pipeline-based or imperative frameworks. It tells a new user exactly what the mental model is.

- **Principle 3** (equations vs. transforms) is the one I'd expect to cause the most confusion for newcomers, so stating it explicitly is the right call. It mirrors the acausal/causal distinction in Modelica/FMI but with a cleaner vocabulary.

- **Principle 7** (replaceable implementations) is well-supported by the codebase — the `Implementation` class, `Backend` protocol, and `ActivatableBackendMixin` all make this concrete. The principle and the architecture are in sync.

- **Principle 10** is a good closing line. It draws the boundary between user responsibility and framework responsibility, which is the question people ask most often.

**Things I'd push back on or refine:**

- **Principle 4** ("Keep the structure close to the engineering") is the vaguest of the ten. "Reflect the concepts and mathematics of the problem rather than computational convenience" is a good aspiration, but it's hard to act on. A modeller reading this might not know *when* they're violating it. You might sharpen it with a concrete anti-pattern, e.g., "Don't introduce a system boundary that exists only to avoid a particular backend limitation."

- **Principle 8** ("Avoid hidden state, accidental broadcasting…") is phrased as a prohibition. The other principles are mostly positive statements. You might reframe it as: "Structure should be inspectable; broadcasting and alignment should be explicit in the model, not inferred silently." That keeps the same intent but matches the tone of the rest.

- **Principle 9** and **Principle 1** overlap somewhat. Both say "the execution path is a consequence of the model, not a separate thing." You could merge them, or make 9 specifically about *debuggability/inspectability* (the "why did Bilt pick this plan?" question) while 1 stays about the *architectural* separation.

- **Principle 6** ("Values carry meaning") is the one I'd expect to be the hardest to enforce in practice. The `Tensor` class and `Quantity`/`Unit` system support it, but the moment someone passes a raw `np.ndarray` through a `BackendCallable.evaluate()`, the meaning is gone. It might be worth acknowledging that this is an *aspiration at the system boundary* rather than a guarantee at every internal step.

**Structural suggestion:**

Ten principles is a lot to hold in a reader's head. The Zen of Python has 19 but they're all one-liners. Yours are 1–3 sentences each, which makes the document feel more like a design rationale than a zen. That's fine, but you might consider whether 6–7 principles, each with a one-line summary and a one-sentence elaboration, would be more memorable. The current 10 could collapse to roughly:

1. Systems define; implementations relate; execution is derived.
2. Describe relationships, not procedures.
3. Equations are acausal; transforms are causal.
4. Structure mirrors the engineering, not the computation.
5. Compose systems; connections are shared quantities.
6. Values carry meaning (units, domain, identity).
7. Implementations are replaceable.
8. Structure is explicit and inspectable.
9. Modellers provide meaning; Bilt handles mechanics.

That's 9, but each is tighter.

**Overall:** it reads like a framework that has been designed with a clear philosophy rather than one that accumulated features and then wrote a philosophy to justify them. That's rarer than it should be. The main risk is that the principles are *aspirational* in places (6, 8) where the implementation is still catching up — and that's fine, as long as the document is honest about that.

No file changes needed here — this is a discussion. Let me know if you'd like to turn any of this into a `zen.md` or `CONTRIBUTING.md` addition, or if you have a different request in mind.

It's a *usable* name, but I think it has a tonal mismatch with the content.

**The problem:** "Zen" connotes lightness, minimalism, and a kind of effortless simplicity. The Zen of Python works because its 19 lines are aphoristic one-liners — "Readability counts," "There should be one obvious way to do it." They're things you can glance at and feel a small emotional resonance.

Bilt's principles are the opposite of light. They're dense, structural, and technical. "Execution is derived from the model." "Equations express relationships; transforms express direction." "Structural analysis, equation isolation, broadcasting, lowering." This is a *design rationale*, not a koan. Calling it "zen" sets an expectation of brevity and ease that the content doesn't deliver, and the gap can make the document feel like it's trying too hard to be approachable.

**Other issues:**

- **Cultural weight.** "Zen" carries a specific philosophical tradition. For a framework whose principles are about acausal modeling, bipartite graph search, and domain alignment, the reference feels decorative rather than earned. It's the kind of name that works for a linter or a formatter, not for a systems-modeling framework.
- **Discoverability.** If someone searches for "Bilt design philosophy" or "Bilt principles," they won't find "Zen of Bilt." The name doesn't describe the content.
- **Tone with the audience.** Bilt's audience is people doing engineering modeling — thermodynamics, fluid dynamics, control systems. They respond to precision. "Zen" signals a different community.

**What I'd consider instead:**

| Name | Why |
|---|---|
| **The Bilt Principles** | Neutral, clear, no baggage. Standard in the engineering world. |
| **Design Philosophy** | What it actually is. Unambiguous. |
| **The Bilt Way** | Implies a shared culture and approach. Slightly warmer than "Principles" without the lightness of "Zen." |
| **First Principles** | Nods to the physics/engineering audience. "First principles" is a term they already use. |
| **The Bilt Manifesto** | Stronger, more declarative. Good if the document is meant to be a statement of intent rather than a gentle guide. |

**If you want to keep "Zen":**

It's not *wrong*, and the PEP 20 reference is real. But I'd only keep it if you also restructure the content to match the format — short, punchy, one-line aphorisms, maybe 12–15 of them, with the dense elaboration moved to a separate "Design Rationale" document. Right now the content is a philosophy paper wearing a zen costume.

**My recommendation:** "The Bilt Principles" for the document title, with a one-line tagline that captures the spirit:

> *Systems define the model. Implementations provide its relationships. Execution is derived.*

That's your principle 1, and it's the single best line in the whole list. It should be the headline, not buried at the top of a numbered list.

No file changes needed — this is a naming discussion. Let me know if you'd like me to draft a `zen.md` or `principles.md` with a restructured version, or if you have a different request.

Here's a rewrite in a tighter, aphoristic style — one line each, parallel in rhythm, closer to the Zen of Python in feel:

zen.md
```
# The Zen of Bilt

The model is declared. Execution is derived.

State what is true, not how to compute it.

Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal.

Structure mirrors the engineering, not the computation.

Compose systems. Connect quantities, not data.

A value is not an array. It is a quantity with a name, a unit, and a domain.

One system, many implementations.

No hidden state. No silent broadcasting. No magic.

The plan is inspectable. The route is explainable.

You define the world. Bilt finds the way through it.
```

**What changed and why:**

- **Each principle is now a single line.** No elaboration, no subordinate clauses. The reader can scan the whole document in five seconds.
- **Parallel structure.** Most lines use a two-part contrast ("X is Y. Z is W.") which gives the document a rhythmic, almost liturgical quality.
- **Jargon is minimized.** "Structural analysis, equation isolation, broadcasting, lowering" is gone. The reader who doesn't know those terms can still feel the intent.
- **The last line is a closing statement**, not a principle. It's the emotional summary: the division of labour between modeller and framework.
- **Principle 8** ("No hidden state. No silent broadcasting. No magic.") uses tricolon for punch. It's the one line that's a prohibition, which is fine — it's the "don't" in a document that's mostly "do."

#### are there alternative useful styles for this doc?  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user is asking about alternative styles for the zen.md document. They want to explore different formats/tones beyond the current aphoristic style. Let me think about what other styles could work for a design philosophy document in a software framework.

Current style: Short declarative aphorisms, one or two sentences each, no elaboration. Like Zen of Python.

Alternative styles I could suggest:

1. **Q&A / Rhetorical** - Each principle framed as a question and answer. "What is a system? A boundary. What is an implementation? A relationship." This is more conversational and guides the reader through understanding.

2. **Contrast / Antithesis** - Each principle as a "not X, but Y" structure. More aggressive, more memorable. "Not pipelines, but systems. Not data flow, but value sharing."

3. **Imperative / Command** - Like a set of rules or a constitution. "Declare the model. Derive the execution." More authoritative.

4. **Narrative / Story** - A short paragraph that tells the story of what happens when you use Bilt. More prose, less list.

5. **Two-column / Table** - "What you do" vs "What Bilt does" - a division of labour table.

6. **Constitutional / Legal** - "Article 1: The model is declared..." More formal, more weighty.

7. **Poetic / Verse** - More rhythmic, almost like a poem. Less structured.

8. **Manifesto** - Longer, more passionate, with a preamble and then the principles.

I think the most useful alternatives to present are:
- The current aphoristic style (already done)
- A "contrast" style (not X, but Y)
- A "question-answer" style
- A "manifesto" style with a short preamble

</thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

Here are three alternative styles, each as a separate file so you can compare them side by side.

**Style 1: Contrast (antithesis)** — each line is a "Not X. Y." pair. More aggressive, more memorable, reads like a manifesto.

zen-contrast.md
```
# The Zen of Bilt

Not a pipeline. A model.

Not a procedure. A relationship.

Not acausal. Causal.
Not causal. Acausal.
Equations for the first. Transforms for the second.

Not computational convenience. Engineering structure.

Not a pipeline of stages. A composition of systems.
Not data flow. Value sharing.

Not a bare array. A named value.
Not a number. A quantity.
Not a shape. A domain.

Not one implementation. Many.

Not hidden state. Not silent broadcasting. Not magic.

Not a black box. An inspectable plan. An explainable route.

Not Bilt's world. Yours.
Not your mechanics. Bilt's.
```

**Style 2: Question and Answer** — each principle is a question followed by a one-line answer. More conversational, guides a new reader through the mental model.

zen-qa.md
```
# The Zen of Bilt

What do you declare? The model.
What do you derive? The execution.

What do you state? What is true.
What do you omit? How to compute it.

What is an equation? Acausal.
What is a transform? Causal.

What should structure mirror? The engineering.
What should it not mirror? The computation.

What do you compose? Systems.
What do you connect? Values, not data.

What is a value? Named.
What is a quantity? Unitful.
What is a tensor? Dimensional.

How many implementations per system? Many.

What is forbidden? Hidden state. Silent broadcasting. Magic.

What must the plan be? Inspectable.
What must the route be? Explainable.

Who defines the world? You.
Who finds the way through it? Bilt.
```

**Style 3: Manifesto** — a short prose preamble, then the principles as a numbered list with one-sentence elaboration. More formal, more weighty, reads like a design document.

zen-manifesto.md
```
# The Zen of Bilt

Bilt is acausal. You declare a model of the world; Bilt derives how to execute it.
This is not a pipeline. This is not a dataflow graph. This is a model, and the
execution is a consequence of the model, not a separate artefact.

1. The model is declared. Execution is derived.
   You supply the structure and the relationships. Bilt finds the route.

2. State what is true, not how to compute it.
   The modeller's job is to assert relationships, not to sequence operations.

3. Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal.
   Use equations where direction is arbitrary. Use transforms where direction is intrinsic.

4. Structure mirrors the engineering, not the computation.
   A system boundary exists because the physics demands it, not because a backend needs it.

5. Compose systems. Connect values, not data.
   A connection is a shared quantity, not a wire carrying a tensor from one stage to the next.

6. A value has a name. A quantity has a unit. A tensor has a domain.
   Meaning is intrinsic to the value, not metadata bolted onto an array.

7. One system, many implementations.
   The same system can be realised by different equations, methods, or backends.

8. No hidden state. No silent broadcasting. No magic.
   If it is not in the model, it is not in the execution.

9. The plan is inspectable. The route is explainable.
   You can always ask why Bilt chose the relationships it did.

10. You define the world. Bilt finds the way through it.
    The division of labour is total. You own meaning. Bilt owns mechanics.
```

**Tradeoffs:**

| Style | Strength | Weakness |
|---|---|---|
| **Aphoristic** (current) | Scannable, memorable, timeless | Can feel vague without context |
| **Contrast** | Punchy, opinionated, good for a README banner | The "Not acausal. Causal." line is confusing without the next line; harder to parse |
| **Q&A** | Onboarding-friendly, guides a new reader | Feels less authoritative; harder to quote a single line in a talk |
| **Manifesto** | Self-contained, no external context needed | Longest; loses the "glance and absorb" quality |

My recommendation: keep the **aphoristic** version as `zen.md` (it's the best for a quick reference), and add the **manifesto** version as `docs/design.md` or `docs/philosophy.md` for newcomers who need the elaboration. The two complement each other.

Want me to commit any of these, or mix elements between them?
