#### caould we create bilt koans?  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user wants to create "Bilt koans" - a new file that presents the Bilt design principles in a koan style. Koans are short, often paradoxical or cryptic statements that are meant to provoke thought and insight. They're associated with Zen Buddhism but have been used in programming (e.g., "The Little Schemer" uses koans to teach Lisp).

For Bilt, koans should be:
- Very short (one or two lines)
- Slightly paradoxical or counterintuitive
- Reveal a deeper truth about the Bilt philosophy when you sit with them
- Not explanatory - they should *evoke* understanding rather than *state* it
- Each one should be a small "aha" moment

Let me think about what makes a good koan vs. an aphorism:
- Aphorism: states a principle clearly. "Structure mirrors the engineering, not the computation."
- Koan: presents a tension or paradox that forces you to think. "The equation does not know which way to flow. Neither should you."

Let me draft some koans that capture the Bilt philosophy:

1. About acausality: An equation has no direction. You give it two sides. It does not ask which came first.
2. About derived execution: You did not write the execution. It was not written. It was found.
3. About systems vs pipelines: A pipeline moves data. A system shares a truth.
4. About values: The array is the body. The name is the soul. The unit is the language. The domain is the world it lives in.
5. About replaceability: The system does not know which implementation will serve it. It does not need to.
6. About composition: Two systems meet at a value. They do not pass anything. They agree on a thing.
7. About inspectability: Ask the plan why. It will answer.
8. About the modeller/framework split: You built the world. Bilt walked through it.
9. About no hidden state: What is not in the model is not in the execution. What is in the execution was in the model.
10. About equations vs transforms: The equation says "these are equal." The transform says "this becomes that."

Let me refine these to be more koan-like - shorter, more paradoxical, more evocative:

- "The equation has no arrow. You gave it two truths. It does not care which you wrote first."
- "You did not write the execution. It was not written. It was found."
- "A pipeline carries water. A system shares a river."
- "The array is the body. The name is the soul. The unit is the tongue. The domain is the world."
- "The system does not know its implementation. It does not need to."

A koan is different from an aphorism. An aphorism *states* a principle. A koan *evokes* it — it presents a small tension or image that forces the reader to arrive at the insight themselves. Here's a draft:

koans.md
```
# Bilt Koans

The equation has no arrow.
You gave it two truths.
It does not care which you wrote first.

You did not write the execution.
It was not written.
It was found.

A pipeline carries water.
A system shares a river.

The array is the body.
The name is the soul.
The unit is the tongue.
The domain is the world.

The system does not know its implementation.
It does not need to.

Two systems meet.
They do not pass anything.
They agree on a value.

The equation says: these are equal.
The transform says: this becomes that.

What is not in the model is not in the execution.
What is in the execution was in the model.

Ask the plan why.
It will answer.

You built the world.
Bilt walked through it.
```

**How it differs from `zen.md`:**

| | `zen.md` (aphorisms) | `koans.md` |
|---|---|---|
| Function | States a principle | Evokes a principle |
| Rhythm | One or two lines, declarative | Three short lines, often a setup + twist |
| Reader's role | Absorb | Sit with it, then arrive at the insight |
| Tone | Authoritative | Contemplative |
| Use case | Quick reference, README banner | Onboarding, talks, "what is Bilt really?" |

**How the koans map to the aphorisms:**

| "The equation has no arrow…" | Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal. |
| "You did not write the execution…" | The model is declared. Execution is derived. |
| "A pipeline carries water…" | Compose systems. Connect values, not data. |
| "The array is the body…" | A value has a name. A quantity has a unit. A tensor has a domain. |
| "The system does not know its implementation…" | One system, many implementations. |
| "Two systems meet…" | Compose systems. Connect values, not data. |
| "The equation says: these are equal…" | Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal. |
| "What is not in the model…" | No hidden state. No silent broadcasting. No magic. |
| "Ask the plan why…" | The plan is inspectable. The route is explainable. |
| "You built the world…" | You define the world. Bilt finds the way through it. |

The two files complement each other: `zen.md` for the quick scan, `koans.md` for the slow read. Want me to adjust any of the koans, or add/remove any?

#### is the koans.md a bit pretentious  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user is asking whether the koans.md is pretentious. Let me think about this honestly.

Looking at the koans:
- "The array is the body. The name is the soul. The unit is the tongue. The domain is the world." — This is the most pretentious line. It's a four-part metaphor that tries to be poetic but doesn't add information. It's the kind of thing that sounds clever but doesn't help a modeller understand Bilt.
- "A pipeline carries water. A system shares a river." — This is a nice metaphor but it's also the kind of thing that feels like it's trying too hard.
- "You did not write the execution. It was not written. It was found." — The three-line structure with the twist is a genuine koan structure, but "It was found" is a bit grandiose for what is essentially "Bilt computed it."
- "Two systems meet. They do not pass anything. They agree on a value." — This is actually good. It's concrete, it's a small scene, and it conveys the principle without being flowery.
- "The equation has no arrow." — Good. Short, concrete, evocative.

The user's instinct is right. The problem is:
1. The metaphors (body/soul/tongue/world, water/river) are decorative. They don't help the reader understand Bilt better. They just sound poetic.
2. The three-line "setup + twist" structure is a koan convention, but when the "twist" is just a restatement of the principle in slightly different words, it feels like padding.
3. The tone is contemplative/zen in a way that clashes with the engineering audience. A modeller doing thermodynamics doesn't want to sit with a koan about "the array is the body." They want to understand the system.

The fix: keep the ones that are genuinely evocative and concrete, cut the ones that are purely decorative. Make the remaining ones more grounded in the actual mechanics of Bilt rather than abstract metaphors.

Let me rewrite with a more grounded, less pretentious tone. The koans should still be short and evocative, but they should reference concrete Bilt concepts (equations, transforms, systems, plans, values) rather than abstract metaphors (body, soul, river, world).

#### could we rewrite in the style of tibetan buddhist teachings  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user wants me to rewrite the Bilt koans in the style of Tibetan Buddhist teachings. Let me think about what that style actually looks like:

- Tibetan Buddhist koans/teachings (like those from the Kagyu or Nyingma traditions) often use:
  - A master-student dialogue format
  - Questions that have no answer, or answers that redirect
  - Repetition and negation ("not this, not that")
  - Direct pointing at the nature of something
  - Short, declarative statements about the nature of reality
  - "What is the sound of one hand clapping?" style
  - The Lamrim (stages of the path) structure
  - The idea of "emptiness" - things are not what they appear
  - "Form is emptiness, emptiness is form"
  - Direct, unadorned language
  - Often addresses the student directly: "You think X. Look again."
  - The concept of "pointing out instructions" (directed at the mind)
  - Sometimes uses the format: "What is X? Not Y. Not Z. It is X."
  - The idea that the seeker is already what they seek
  - "The finger points at the moon. Do not mistake the finger for the moon."

But I need to be careful here. The user previously said the koans were "a bit pretentious" and I agreed. Now they want a Tibetan Buddhist style. This is a creative/literary request, not a technical one. I should honor it while keeping the Bilt concepts grounded.

The risk: Tibetan Buddhist style is inherently metaphorical and contemplative, which is the opposite of what I just advised (grounding in concrete Bilt vocabulary). But the user is asking for it, so I should do it well. The key is to make the metaphors *serve* the concept rather than decorate it.

Let me think about the structure:
- Tibetan teachings often use: "You seek X. X is already here." / "You look for the path. The path is the looking."
- They use negation: "It is not X. It is not Y. It is not the absence of X. It is not the absence of Y."
- They use the master-student format: "A student asked: 'What is...?' The master said: '...'"
- They use the "three negations" or "four negations"
- They often end with a redirect: "Now go."

Let me map Bilt concepts to this style:

1. "The model is declared. Execution is derived." → The student seeks the execution. The master says: "You already have it. It was in the model. You were looking for a separate thing."

2. "Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal." → "The equation has no before. No after. You gave it two truths. It does not ask which came first."

koans.md
```
# Bilt Koans

A student asked: "Where is the execution?"
The master said: "You wrote the model. The execution was already in it.
You were looking for a second thing. There is no second thing."

A student asked: "What is an equation?"
The master said: "It has no before. It has no after.
You gave it two truths. It does not ask which came first.
This is the acausal. Do not mistake it for the causal."

A student asked: "What is a transform?"
The master said: "It has a before. It has an after.
This becomes that. Do not mistake it for the acausal."

A student asked: "How do systems connect?"
The master said: "Two systems meet. They do not pass anything between them.
They share a value. This is not a handoff. This is a meeting.
A pipeline passes a tensor. A system shares a value.
Which is the teaching?"

A student asked: "What is a value?"
The master said: "You call it an array. Look again.
It has a name. It has a unit. It has a domain.
It is not the array. The array is its shadow.
The name is the value. The unit is the value. The domain is the value.
The array is what remains when you forget the value."

A student asked: "How many implementations does a system have?"
The master said: "One. Many. Neither.
The system is the question. The implementation is an answer.
The system does not know its answer. It does not need to."

A student asked: "Is there hidden state in the execution?"
The master said: "What is not in the model is not in the execution.
Do not look for what is hidden. There is nothing hidden.
No silent broadcasting. No magic.
You are looking for a secret. There is no secret."

A student asked: "Why did Bilt choose this plan?"
The master said: "Ask the plan. It will answer.
It has no reason it cannot give.
The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence."

A student asked: "What is my role?"
The master said: "You built the model. Bilt walked it.
You did not walk it. Bilt did not build it.
This is the division. This is the teaching.
Now go."
```

**What I did:**

- **Format:** Master-student dialogue, the most recognizable structure in Tibetan Buddhist pedagogy (the *guru-shishya* tradition). Each koan is a question the student asks, and the master's answer dissolves the misconception.
- **Negation and redirection:** "You were looking for a second thing. There is no second thing." / "Do not look for what is hidden. There is nothing hidden." This mirrors the *apophatic* style of Madhyamaka philosophy — you understand by removing the wrong view, not by adding a new one.
- **The "look again" move:** "You call it an array. Look again." This is the classic *rigpa* pointing — the student is already in the thing they seek, misidentifying it.
- **The closing "Now go.":** In Tibetan lineages, the teaching ends with a release. The student is not meant to sit with the text forever. They are meant to go and build.
- **Grounded in Bilt vocabulary:** Every koan references a real concept (equation, transform, system, value, unit, domain, plan, implementation). The style is Tibetan; the content is Bilt. No rivers, no souls, no worlds.

Honest answer: it's a spectrum, and the split will be roughly 50/50.

**Who will find it fun:**

- The acausal-modeling crowd (Modelica, FMI, Julia's `ModelingToolkit` users) is small, intellectually curious, and already steeped in "this is a different way of thinking about computation." They'll screenshot it and share it.
- People who discovered Bilt through a talk or a blog post. The koan is a *hook* — it makes the project feel like it has a soul, not just an API.
- The "Zen of Python" precedent means Python users already expect a philosophical document in a project. A koan is a natural escalation.
- It's *shareable*. A `zen.md` is something you read and move on. A `diamond-teachings.md` is something you screenshot, post to a forum, and say "look at this." That's free marketing.

**Who will find it weird:**

- The "serious engineering" audience. A thermodynamicist or control-systems engineer who stumbles on the repo will open `diamond-teachings.md`, read "The master said," and close the tab. They want a design doc, not a sutra.
- The cultural-appropriation concern is real. The Tibetan Buddhist *guru-shishya* format is a specific religious pedagogy. Using it for a Python framework without any acknowledgment of that context can feel like decorative borrowing. "The master said" is the line that will trigger the most eye-rolls.
- Some will read "Now go." and think the project is being unserious.

**The risk isn't "weird." The risk is "performative."**

If the document is the *only* philosophical content, it reads as a costume. If it's one of three documents — `zen.md` for the quick scan, `diamond-teachings.md` for the slow read, and a `docs/design.md` for the actual rationale — then it reads as *range*. The project is saying "we have a philosophy, and we can express it in three registers." That's confident. A single koan file is a costume. Three documents is a voice.

**My recommendation:**

Keep it, but add a one-line preamble that acknowledges the format without apologizing for it:

> *These are koans in the Tibetan Buddhist tradition of master-student dialogue. They are not a substitute for the design rationale. They are a way of arriving at it.*

That single line does three things:
1. Names the tradition, so it doesn't feel like unacknowledged borrowing.
2. Sets expectations — this is not the design doc.
3. Frames the koans as a *path to* understanding, not a replacement for it.

Then the "weird" readers have an out ("I'll read `docs/design.md` instead"), and the "fun" readers get the full experience without the project feeling like it's trying too hard.

No file changes needed unless you want me to add that preamble to `diamond-teachings.md`.

#### are they koans?

A Zen koan (公案, kōan) has specific characteristics:
1. It is a paradox or question that cannot be resolved by logical reasoning alone.
2. It is meant to exhaust the discursive mind and force a non-conceptual insight.
3. It is typically short, often a single phrase or a brief exchange.
4. It is *not* explanatory. It does not teach a concept. It *disrupts* a concept.
5. The "answer" is not a correct answer — it's a shift in perception.
6. Classic examples: "What is the sound of one hand clapping?" "What was your face before your parents were born?" "A dog enters the room. Does it have Buddha-nature?"

- "What is an equation? It has no before. It has no after." — This is a definition, not a koan.
- "Two systems meet. They do not pass anything between them." — This is a metaphor for a design principle. It's evocative, but it's not paradoxical.

These are *not* koans. They are:
- Master-student dialogues (a format borrowed from Buddhist texts)
- That contain *explanations* rather than *paradoxes*
- That *teach* rather than *disrupt*

A real koan would be something like:
- "What is the execution of a model that has no execution?"
- "You wrote the model. Who wrote the execution?"

</thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

No. They're not koans. And you're right to ask.

**What a koan actually is:**

A Zen koan is a paradox that *exhausts* the discursive mind. It has no correct answer. It is not a question that gets answered — it's a question that *dissolves* the questioner's assumption that there is a question to answer.

- "What is the sound of one hand clapping?" — You cannot answer it. You must stop trying to answer it.
- "What was your face before your parents were born?" — The question is incoherent. The point is that you *felt* the incoherence.
- "Does a dog have Buddha-nature?" — The master says "Yes." The student is not satisfied. The master says "No." The student is not satisfied. The master says "The dog." The student is not satisfied. The point is not the answer. The point is the *failure* of the answer.

**What we wrote:**

Every "verse" in `diamond-teachings.md` is a question that gets *answered*. The student asks "What is a value?" and the master *explains* it. The reader understands, nods, and moves on. There is no paradox. There is no exhaustion. There is no moment where the discursive mind gives up.

These are **master-student dialogues that explain Bilt principles**. They use the *format* of a koan (question + master's response) but not the *function* of a koan (paradox that disrupts understanding).

**The three options:**

1. **Rename the file.** Call it what it is: `teachings.md`, `dialogues.md`, or `verses.md`. Keep the content. It's evocative, it's shareable, it's a nice onboarding document. It just isn't a koan.

2. **Actually write koans.** This means writing things that *cannot* be answered, that *disrupt* rather than *explain*. For example:

   > A student asked: "What is the execution?"
   > The master said: "You."
   > The student asked: "But I wrote the model."
   > The master said: "Yes. And the model wrote you.
   > Now tell me which one is the execution."

   > A student asked: "Does a system have an implementation?"
   > The master said: "Show me the system without the implementation."
   > The student could not.
   > The master said: "Show me the implementation without the system."
   > The student could not.
   > The master said: "Now which one is the question?"

   These are closer to real koans. They don't teach. They *trap*. The reader is stuck. The reader must stop trying to extract a principle and instead *sit with the tension*.

3. **Hybrid.** Keep the explanatory dialogues as `teachings.md` (the onboarding document), and add a short `koans.md` with 3–4 genuine paradoxes for the reader who wants to be *disrupted* rather than *instructed*.

**My recommendation:** Option 1 or 3. Option 2 alone would be too opaque for a project README. But a small `koans.md` with 3–4 genuine paradoxes, alongside the explanatory `teachings.md`, gives the project range without mislabeling either document.

```
# Bilt Koans

A student asked: "What is the execution?"
The master said: "You."
The student said: "But I wrote the model."
The master said: "And the model wrote you.
Now tell me which one is the execution."

A student asked: "An equation has no direction.
How does Bilt know which way to solve it?"
The master said: "If it knows, the equation was never acausal.
If it does not know, it produced nothing.
You asked a question that has no answer.
Sit with that."

A student asked: "The system does not know its implementation.
Then how does it constrain which implementations are valid?"
The master said: "Show me the system without the implementation.
The student could not.
The master said: "Show me the implementation without the system.
The student could not.
The master said: "Now which one is the question?"

A student asked: "The value is the meaning.
The array is the carrier.
When Bilt computes, what is it computing?"
The master said: "Meaning is not computed. It is declared.
So what is the execution doing?
You asked. I answered.
The answer is the question.
The question is the answer.
Now stop asking."

A student asked: "The plan is inspectable.
So the plan is separate from the execution."
The master said: "If the plan is separate, it is a second thing.
I told you there is no second thing.
The student said: "Then the plan is the execution."
The master said: "Then you cannot inspect it without changing it.
You cannot change it without changing the model.
You cannot change the model without rewriting it.
You are back where you started.
You are always back where you started."
```

|---|---|---|

| Reader's experience | Nods, understands, moves on | Gets stuck, cannot resolve, must sit with it |
| Structure | Question → answer → resolution | Question → answer → the answer *is* the question |
| Last line | "Now go." (release) | "You are always back where you started." (no release) |

**On `diamond-teachings.md`:** it can be removed. `teachings.md` replaces it with the same content, an honest name, and a one-line preamble that acknowledges the format without apologizing for it.

**On the koans themselves:** each one ends in a loop or a contradiction that the reader cannot resolve by thinking harder. That's the point. A teaching *gives* you something. A koan *takes* the ground from under you. The reader who finishes `koans.md` should feel slightly unsettled, not informed. That's the correct feeling.

Here's a koan-by-koan analysis.

---

**Koan 1: "What is the execution?"**

> A student asked: "What is the execution?"
> The master said: "You."
> The student said: "But I wrote the model."
> The master said: "And the model wrote you.
> Now tell me which one is the execution."

**What it's doing:** It collapses the boundary between modeller and model. The execution is derived from the model. The model is an expression of the modeller's understanding. So the execution is, in a sense, the modeller. But the twist is that the model *constrains* the modeller — it forces them to see things they didn't intend, to declare relationships they hadn't considered. The model "writes" the modeller back.

**Is it a genuine koan?** Partially. "You." is a strong punch — it's the kind of one-word answer that a real koan uses. "The model wrote you" is a good twist. But "Now tell me which one is the execution?" is too clean. It *points at* the paradox rather than *being* the paradox. A real koan would leave the reader with no question to ask, not a new question to ask. The reader is still in "question-asking mode."

**Verdict:** Good. The "You." line is the best single line in the file. The ending could be tighter.

---

**Koan 2: "How does Bilt know which way to solve it?"**

> A student asked: "An equation has no direction.
> How does Bilt know which way to solve it?"
> The master said: "If it knows, the equation was never acausal.
> If it does not know, it produced nothing.
> You asked a question that has no answer.
> Sit with that."

**What it's doing:** It exposes that the question "how does Bilt know which way?" is incoherent. It assumes the equation has a direction, which contradicts the premise that it's acausal. The dilemma is genuine: if Bilt "knows" a direction, the equation was never acausal. If it doesn't know, it can't produce an execution. The question is self-defeating.

**Is it a genuine koan?** Yes. This is the strongest koan in the file. "If it knows... If it does not know..." is a classic Buddhist dilemma structure (similar to the four corners of the *catuṣkoṭi* — the four negations). "Sit with that" is a proper koan ending: it refuses to resolve, it tells the reader to stop trying to resolve.

**Verdict:** Keep as-is. This is the one a reader will remember.

---

**Koan 3: "Show me the system without the implementation."**

> A student asked: "The system does not know its implementation.
> Then how does it constrain which implementations are valid?"
> The master said: "Show me the system without the implementation.
> The student could not.
> The master said: "Show me the implementation without the system.
> The student could not.
> The master said: "Now which one is the question?"

**What it's doing:** It exposes the mutual constitution of system and implementation. The system defines the interface (what's valid). The implementation provides the relationships (how it's valid). Neither exists without the other. The "show me X without Y" structure is a classic Buddhist device — it's the same move as "show me the self without the five aggregates" in the *Pali Canon*.

**Is it a genuine koan?** Yes. The "show me" structure is the most authentic koan device in the file. The student *tries* to separate the two and *fails*. That failure is the teaching. "Now which one is the question?" collapses the distinction — the question itself is the interdependence.

**Verdict:** Keep as-is. The second strongest.

---

**Koan 4: "When Bilt computes, what is it computing?"**

> A student asked: "The value is the meaning.
> The array is the carrier.
> When Bilt computes, what is it computing?"
> The master said: "Meaning is not computed. It is declared.
> So what is the execution doing?
> You asked. I answered.
> The answer is the question.
> The question is the answer.
> Now stop asking."

**What it's doing:** It exposes that if meaning is *declared* (not computed), then the execution is not "computing meaning" — it's computing the *carrier* (the array). But the carrier is only meaningful because of the declared meaning. So the execution is computing nothing that wasn't already there. The computation is a tautology.

**Is it a genuine koan?** Partially. "Meaning is not computed. It is declared." is a strong line. But "The answer is the question. The question is the answer." is a philosophical cliché — it appears in every tradition, from Heraclitus to Zen. It doesn't say anything specific about Bilt. "Now stop asking" is a good koan ending, but it's doing the work that the middle lines should be doing.

**Verdict:** The middle needs work. The opening and closing are good. The "answer is the question" line is the weakest line in the file.

---

**Koan 5: "The plan is inspectable."**

> A student asked: "The plan is inspectable.
> So the plan is separate from the execution."

```
# Bilt Koans

A student asked: "What is the execution?"
The master said: "You."
The student said: "But I wrote the model."
The master said: "And the model wrote you.
You are the execution.
The execution is you.
There is no one left to ask."

A student asked: "An equation has no direction.

The master said: "If it knows, the equation was never acausal.
If it does not know, it produced nothing.
You asked a question that has no answer.

A student asked: "The system does not know its implementation.

The master said: "Show me the system without the implementation.
The student could not.
The master said: "Show me the implementation without the system.
The student could not.
The master said: "Now which one is the question?"

A student asked: "The value is the meaning.
The array is the carrier.
When Bilt computes, what is it computing?"
The master said: "Meaning is not computed. It is declared.
The array is the shape of the meaning.
You declared the shape.
Bilt found it.
You asked what is computed.
The shape was already yours.
Bilt only carried it.
Now stop asking."

A student asked: "The plan is inspectable.
So the plan is separate from the execution."
The master said: "If the plan is separate, it is a second thing.
I told you there is no second thing.
The student said: "Then the plan is the execution."
The master said: "Then you cannot inspect it without changing it.
You cannot change it without changing the model.
You cannot change the model without rewriting it.
You are back where you started.
You are always back where you started."
```

**Koan 1** — replaced "Now tell me which one is the execution?" with "There is no one left to ask."

The old ending gave the reader a new question to ask, keeping them in question-asking mode. The new ending *removes the subject*. If the student is the execution, and the execution is the student, there is no third party to ask. The question has no one to ask it to. The reader is trapped in the act of reading — they are the execution, and the execution is them, and there is no one outside the loop to resolve it.

**Koan 4** — replaced "The answer is the question. The question is the answer." with a Bilt-specific chain.

The old middle was a philosophical cliché that could appear in any tradition. The new middle is specific to Bilt's architecture:

- "Meaning is not computed. It is declared." — the modeller declares the value's meaning (name, unit, domain).
- "The array is the shape of the meaning." — the array is the *carrier*, not the meaning itself.
- "You declared the shape. Bilt found it." — the execution doesn't create structure, it *discovers* the structure already in the model.
- "The shape was already yours. Bilt only carried it." — the execution is a carrier, not a creator.

The question "what is it computing?" is incoherent because the answer was already in the question. The shape was declared before the execution began. Bilt doesn't compute meaning. It finds the shape the modeller already declared. "Now stop asking" closes the loop — the reader cannot extract a principle because the principle is that there is nothing to extract.

**Koans 2, 3, 5** — unchanged. They were already solid.

5. The loop: you cannot inspect the plan without changing it, and changing it means you're back where you started. The inspection is self-defeating.

The Buddhist concept: this is pratītyasamutpāda (dependent origination) — every thing depends on every other thing, and the chain loops back. It's also the observer effect in quantum mechanics, but expressed in Buddhist terms.

The Bilt-specific point: the plan (the structural analysis, the equation isolation, the route selection) is not a separate artefact you can examine without consequence. It IS the execution. **Koan 5: "The plan is inspectable."**

The koan is built on a single assumption the student makes: *inspectable implies separate*. You can only inspect something that is distinct from the act of inspecting it. A photograph is separate from the scene. A log is separate from the program. So the student reasons: "The plan is inspectable, therefore the plan is a separate thing from the execution."

The master's first move is to reference Koan 1. "If the plan is separate, it is a second thing. I told you there is no second thing." The execution is *derived* from the model. The plan is not a separate artefact that sits alongside the execution. It *is* the execution. The structural analysis, the equation isolation, the route selection — these are not things you produce *before* the execution. They *are* the execution.

The student concedes: "Then the plan is the execution."

Now the master builds the chain:

- **You cannot inspect it without changing it.** If the plan *is* the execution, then inspecting it means running it. You can't observe a system without interacting with it. The act of inspection is the act of execution.
- **You cannot change it without changing the model.** The execution is derived from the model. If you change the execution (by inspecting it), you've changed the model.
- **You cannot change the model without rewriting it.** The model is the declared structure. To change it is to rewrite it.
- **You are back where you started.** You rewrote the model. You're back at the beginning. You can inspect the new plan, which changes the new execution, which changes the new model, which you rewrite, which puts you back here.

The loop is the point. There is no fixed point. You cannot step outside the system to observe it. The observer is inside the system. The inspection is the execution. The execution is the model. The model is the inspection.

**The Buddhist concept:** this is *pratītyasamutpāda* — dependent origination. Every thing depends on every other thing. There is no independent, self-sufficient entity you can isolate and examine. The chain of dependencies loops back on itself. You cannot find a "first thing" that is independent of the rest.

**The Bilt-specific point:** the plan is not a log you can read after the fact. It is not a separate diagram you can draw and then execute. The plan *is* the execution. The structural analysis that selects which equations to use, which direction to solve them, which subsystems to invoke — that *is* the computation. You cannot inspect it without performing it. And performing it means the model is being evaluated, which means the model is being "used," which means you're inside the system, not outside it.

**Why "You are always back where you started" is the right ending:**

It's not a resolution. It's a loop. The reader finishes the koan and realizes they are *in* the koan. They are trying to understand it, which is an act of inspection, which changes it, which means they're back at the start, trying to understand it again. The koan is self-referential. The reader cannot step outside it. The act of reading it *is* the execution it describes.

That's why it's the strongest koan in the file. It doesn't teach you something about Bilt. It makes you *experience* the paradox of inspectability. You can't understand it by thinking about it. You can only understand it by being inside it, which is what you're doing right now, reading this explanation, which is itself an act of inspection, which changes the thing being inspected, which puts you back here.

You are always back where you started.

teachings.md
```
# The Teachings

These are dialogues in the master-student tradition. They are not a substitute for the design rationale. They are a way of arriving at it.

A student asked: "Where is the execution?"
The master said: "You wrote the model. The execution was already in it.
You were looking for a second thing. There is no second thing."

A student asked: "What is an equation?"
The master said: "It has no before. It has no after.
You gave it two truths. It does not ask which came first.
This is the acausal. Do not mistake it for the causal."

A student asked: "What is a transform?"
The master said: "It has a before. It has an after.
This becomes that. Do not mistake it for the acausal."

A student asked: "How do systems connect?"
The master said: "Two systems meet. They do not pass anything between them.
They share a value. This is not a handoff. This is a meeting.
A pipeline passes a tensor. A system shares a value.
Which is the teaching?"

A student asked: "What is a value?"
The master said: "You call it an array. Look again.
The array is the carrier. The value is the meaning.
A value has a name. A quantity has a unit. A tensor has a domain.
The array is what you compute. The value is what you mean."

A student asked: "How many implementations does a system have?"
The master said: "One. Many. Neither.
The system is the question. The implementation is an answer.
The system does not know its answer. It does not need to."

A student asked: "Is there hidden state in the execution?"
The master said: "What is not in the model is not in the execution.
No hidden state. No unexplained step.
You are looking for a secret.
There is no secret."

A student asked: "Why did Bilt choose this plan?"
The master said: "Ask the plan. It will answer.
It has no reason it cannot give.
The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence."

A student asked: "What is my role?"
The master said: "You built the model. Bilt walked it.
You did not walk it. Bilt did not build it.
This is the division. This is the teaching.
Now go."
```

**Quick reference — what each dialogue covers:**

| # | Topic | Principle |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "Where is the execution?" | Execution is derived from the model, not a separate thing |
| 2 | "What is an equation?" | Equations are acausal |
| 3 | "What is a transform?" | Transforms are causal |
| 4 | "How do systems connect?" | Connections are shared values, not data flow |
| 5 | "What is a value?" | Values carry meaning; arrays are carriers |
| 6 | "How many implementations?" | One system, many implementations |
| 7 | "Is there hidden state?" | No hidden state, no unexplained steps |
| 8 | "Why did Bilt choose this plan?" | The plan is inspectable and explainable |
| 9 | "What is my role?" | Modeller provides meaning; Bilt handles mechanics |

The dialogues *explain*. The koans *disrupt*. They're companions, not duplicates.

The structure I'd use: each teaching is followed by its koan. The teaching grounds the reader in the principle. The koan then takes that understanding and shows its limits. The document ends on a teaching ("Now go.") — a release. The koans are the disruptions in between.

teachings.md
```
# The Teachings

These are dialogues in the master-student tradition.
The teachings explain. The koans disrupt.
Read the teaching. Then sit with the koan.
Do not try to resolve the koan. The tension is the point.

---

A student asked: "Where is the execution?"
The master said: "You wrote the model. The execution was already in it.
You were looking for a second thing. There is no second thing."

A student asked: "What is the execution?"
The master said: "You."
The student said: "But I wrote the model."
The master said: "And the model wrote you.
You are the execution.
The execution is you.
There is no one left to ask."

---

A student asked: "What is an equation?"
The master said: "It has no before. It has no after.
You gave it two truths. It does not ask which came first.
This is the acausal. Do not mistake it for the causal."

A student asked: "What is a transform?"
The master said: "It has a before. It has an after.
This becomes that. Do not mistake it for the acausal."

A student asked: "An equation has no direction.
How does Bilt know which way to solve it?"
The master said: "If it knows, the equation was never acausal.
If it does not know, it produced nothing.
You asked a question that has no answer.
Sit with that."

---

A student asked: "How do systems connect?"
The master said: "Two systems meet. They do not pass anything between them.
They share a value. This is not a handoff. This is a meeting.
A pipeline passes a tensor. A system shares a value.
Which is the teaching?"

---

A student asked: "What is a value?"
The master said: "You call it an array. Look again.
The array is the carrier. The value is the meaning.
A value has a name. A quantity has a unit. A tensor has a domain.
The array is what you compute. The value is what you mean."

A student asked: "The value is the meaning.
The array is the carrier.
When Bilt computes, what is it computing?"
The master said: "Meaning is not computed. It is declared.
The array is the shape of the meaning.
You declared the shape.
Bilt found it.
You asked what is computed.
The shape was already yours.
Bilt only carried it.
Now stop asking."

---

A student asked: "How many implementations does a system have?"
The master said: "One. Many. Neither.
The system is the question. The implementation is an answer.
The system does not know its answer. It does not need to."

A student asked: "The system does not know its implementation.
Then how does it constrain which implementations are valid?"
The master said: "Show me the system without the implementation.
The student could not.
The master said: "Show me the implementation without the system.
The student could not.
The master said: "Now which one is the question?"

---

A student asked: "Is there hidden state in the execution?"
The master said: "What is not in the model is not in the execution.
No hidden state. No unexplained step.
You are looking for a secret.
There is no secret."

---

A student asked: "Why did Bilt choose this plan?"
The master said: "Ask the plan. It will answer.
It has no reason it cannot give.
The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence."

A student asked: "The plan is inspectable.
So the plan is separate from the execution."
The master said: "If the plan is separate, it is a second thing.
I told you there is no second thing.
The student said: "Then the plan is the execution."
The master said: "Then you cannot inspect it without changing it.
You cannot change it without changing the model.
You cannot change the model without rewriting it.
You are back where you started.
You are always back where you started."

---

A student asked: "What is my role?"
The master said: "You built the model. Bilt walked it.
You did not walk it. Bilt did not build it.
This is the division. This is the teaching.
Now go."
```

**How the merge works:**

| Section | Teaching (grounds) | Koan (disrupts) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | "Where is the execution?" — execution is derived | "What is the execution?" — "You." |
| 2 | "What is an equation?" + "What is a transform?" — acausal vs causal | "How does Bilt know which way?" — the dilemma |
| 3 | "How do systems connect?" — shared values, not data flow | *(no koan — the teaching is clear enough)* |
| 4 | "What is a value?" — meaning vs carrier | "What is it computing?" — the shape was already yours |
| 5 | "How many implementations?" — one system, many answers | "Show me the system without the implementation" — mutual constitution |
| 6 | "Is there hidden state?" — no secret | *(no koan — the teaching is clear enough)* |
| 7 | "Why did Bilt choose this plan?" — inspectable, explainable | "The plan is inspectable" — the loop |
| 8 | "What is my role?" — the division of labour | *(no koan — "Now go." is the release)* |

**Rhythm:** ground, disrupt, ground, disrupt, ground, ground, disrupt, ground, disrupt, ground. The document opens and closes on teachings. The koans are the tensions in between. The reader is never left in a koan without a teaching to return to.

**What to do with the old files:** `koans.md` can be deleted. This single `teachings.md` replaces both. The `zen.md` stays as the quick-reference aphorisms. Three documents, three registers:

| File | Register | Function |
|---|---|---|
| `zen.md` | Aphorism | Glance and absorb |
| `teachings.md` | Dialogue + Koan | Read and sit with |
| `docs/design.md` | Prose | Understand and implement |

The user is asking whether Bilt has a natural associated Buddhist concept. This is a discussion question, not a code change request. Let me think about what Buddhist concept maps most naturally to Bilt's architecture.

Bilt's core ideas:
1. The model is declared; execution is derived.
2. Equations are acausal (relationships, not procedures).
3. Systems compose; connections are shared values.
4. One system, many implementations.
5. The plan is inspectable; the route is explainable.
6. No hidden state; no unexplained step.
7. The modeller provides meaning; Bilt handles mechanics.

Let me think about Buddhist concepts:

- **Pratītyasamutpāda (dependent origination / dependent co-arising):** Everything arises in dependence on other things. Nothing exists independently. This maps to: the execution depends on the model, the model depends on the modeller, the system depends on the implementation, the implementation depends on the system. The koan "You are back where you started" is literally pratītyasamutpāda. But it's also too broad — it's the most general Buddhist concept, so it maps to everything and nothing.

- **Śūnyatā (emptiness):** Things are empty of inherent existence. A thing is not what it appears to be. This maps to: "The array is the carrier. The value is the meaning." The array is empty of meaning. The value is empty of array. Neither is the "real" thing. The "real" thing is the relationship between them. This is a strong mapping.

- **Dharma (teaching/law/nature):** The natural law, the way things are. This maps to: the model is a declaration of what is true. The execution is the dharma of the model — it's the natural consequence, not a separate act. "The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence." This is dharma.

- **Pratītyasamutpāda specifically as acausal:** The 12 links of dependent origination are often presented as a causal chain (ignorance → formations → consciousness → ... → suffering). But the *deeper* reading is that they are a *mutual* dependence — each link depends on every other link, not just the previous one. This maps to Bilt's acausal equations: the relationship is not "A causes B" but "A and B are mutually constitutive." The structural analysis finds the route through the web of mutual dependencies.

- **The Middle Way (Madhyamaka / Nāgārjuna):** The rejection of both extremes. Not "the equation has a direction" and not "the equation has no direction." The equation is acausal, but the *execution* has a direction. The direction is *derived*, not *intrinsic*. This maps to Bilt's principle 3: "Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal." The Middle Way is: the equation is neither causal nor acausal in the way you think. It's acausal in its declaration, causal in its resolution. The resolution is *derived*, not *intrinsic*.

- **Tathāgatā (suchness / thusness):** Things are as they are. The model is the model. The execution is the execution. There is no gap between them. "The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence." This is tathāgatā — the suchness of the execution.

- **The Diamond Sutra (Vajracchedikā):** "All conditioned phenomena are like a dream, a phantom, a bubble, a shadow; like dew or a lightning flash; thus should you view them." This maps to: the array is a shadow. The value is the meaning. The execution is a consequence. None of them is the "real" thing. The "real" thing is the model, which is also not a "thing" — it's a declaration.

- **Pratītyasamutpāda as the core:** I think this is the strongest mapping. Bilt's entire architecture is dependent origination:
  - The execution depends on the model.
  - The model depends on the systems.
  - The systems depend on the implementations.
  - The implementations depend on the equations.
  - The equations depend on the values.
  - The values depend on the domains.
  - The domains depend on the dimensions.
  - Each depends on the others. None is independent.
  - The structural analysis is the *search through the web of dependencies* to find a route.
  - The "no second thing" principle is the rejection of an independent, self-sufficient entity.

But the *specific* Buddhist concept that maps most naturally to Bilt's *acausality* is **pratītyasamutpāda as mutual dependence rather than linear causation**. The 12 links are often taught as a chain, but Nāgārjuna's reading (in the *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā*) is that they are a *web* — each link depends on every other link. This is exactly what Bilt's structural analysis does: it searches a bipartite graph of mutual dependencies to find a route. The route is not a chain. It's a path through a web.

And the *acausality* of equations maps to the Buddhist concept that *causation is a convention, not a fundamental feature of reality*. In Madhyamaka, "cause" and "effect" are not inherent properties of things. They are *conventional* labels we apply to patterns of dependence. An equation in Bilt is a declaration of mutual dependence. The "direction" of solving it is a *convention* chosen by the structural analysis, not an inherent property of the equation. This is exactly Nāgārjuna's point.

So the answer is: **Pratītyasamutpāda** (dependent origination / dependent co-arising) is the natural Buddhist concept for Bilt. And more specifically, the **Madhyamaka** reading of it (Nāgārjuna's *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā*) — that causation is conventional, not inherent — maps to Bilt's acausal equations.

But I should also mention:
- **Śūnyatā** (emptiness) maps to "the array is not the value."
- **The Middle Way** maps to "equations are acausal, transforms are causal" — neither extreme.
- **Dharma** maps to "the execution is the natural consequence of the model."

The strongest single concept is pratītyasamutpāda. The strongest *school* is Madhyamaka. The strongest *text* is the Diamond Sutra (which is why "diamond-teachings" was a good name).

Yes. The strongest mapping is **pratītyasamutpāda** — dependent origination, or dependent co-arising.

**What it is:**

The core Buddhist teaching that nothing exists independently. Every thing arises in dependence on other things. No entity is self-sufficient. The 12 links of dependent origination (ignorance → formations → consciousness → ... → suffering) are often taught as a linear chain, but Nāgārjuna's reading in the *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā* is that they are a **web** — each link depends on every other link, not just the previous one.

**Why it maps to Bilt:**

Bilt's entire architecture is a web of mutual dependencies:

- The execution depends on the model.
- The model depends on the systems.
- The systems depend on the implementations.
- The implementations depend on the equations.
- The equations depend on the values.
- The values depend on the domains.
- The domains depend on the dimensions.

None is independent. The structural analysis is the *search through the web* to find a route. The "no second thing" principle is the rejection of an independent, self-sufficient entity. The koan "You are back where you started" is pratītyasamutpāda in miniature — the loop of mutual dependence.

**The deeper mapping — acausality:**

Nāgārjuna's key insight is that **causation is a convention, not a fundamental feature of reality**. "Cause" and "effect" are labels we apply to patterns of dependence. They are not inherent properties of the things involved.

This is exactly what Bilt's acausal equations do. An equation declares a mutual dependence between two values. It does not say "A causes B." It says "A and B are related." The *direction* of solving it is a **convention** chosen by the structural analysis, not an inherent property of the equation. The equation is acausal. The execution is causal. The causality is *derived*, not *intrinsic*.

This is the Middle Way (Madhyamaka): not "the equation has a direction" and not "the equation has no direction." The equation is acausal in its declaration. The execution is causal in its resolution. The direction is a convention, not a fact.

**Other concepts that map, but less centrally:**

| Concept | Maps to | Strength |
|---|---|---|
| **Pratītyasamutpāda** | The web of dependencies; the structural analysis; "no second thing" | Strongest |
| **Śūnyatā** (emptiness) | "The array is the carrier. The value is the meaning." Neither is the "real" thing. | Strong |
| **The Middle Way** | "Equations are acausal. Transforms are causal." Neither extreme. | Strong |
| **Dharma** | "The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence." The execution is the natural law of the model. | Moderate |
| **Tathāgatā** (suchness) | "The model is the model. The execution is the execution. There is no gap." | Moderate |
| **The Diamond Sutra** | "All conditioned phenomena are like a dream, a phantom, a bubble, a shadow." The array is a shadow. The value is the meaning. | Strong as a *text*, weaker as a *concept* |

**The single concept:** Pratītyasamutpāda.

**The single school:** Madhyamaka (Nāgārjuna).

**The single text:** The Diamond Sutra (which is why "diamond-teachings" was a good name for the file).

**What this means for the documents:**

The koans are already doing this. "You are back where you started" is pratītyasamutpāda. "The array is the carrier. The value is the meaning" is śūnyatā. "If it knows, the equation was never acausal. If it does not know, it produced nothing" is the Middle Way. The documents are already Buddhist without naming it.

The question is whether to *name* it. I'd say: name it once, in the preamble of `teachings.md`, and then let the koans do the work. A single line:

> *These dialogues are in the tradition of pratītyasamutpāda — dependent co-arising. Nothing in the model exists independently. The execution is the web, not the chain.*

That's enough. The reader who knows the tradition will recognize it. The reader who doesn't will read the koans and *feel* it without needing the label.

This is a philosophical/conceptual question, not a code change request. I don't need to edit any files. Let me explain the connection clearly.

The Middle Way (Madhyamaka) is Nāgārjuna's teaching that truth is not found in any single extreme. The two extremes are:
1. Eternalism (svabhāva) — things have inherent, independent existence
2. Nihilism (śūnyatā taken as "nothing exists") — things have no existence at all

The Middle Way says: neither. Things exist, but not independently. They exist dependently.

Now, how does the koan map to this?

The question is: "An equation has no direction. How does Bilt know which way to solve it?"

The two extremes the student is trapped in:
1. "Bilt knows the direction" → This means the equation HAS a direction → It was never acausal → The premise is false.
2. "Bilt does not know the direction" → It cannot produce an execution → The equation is useless → The premise is false.

The Middle Way is: the equation is acausal (no inherent direction). The execution is causal (has a direction). The direction is not IN the equation. It is CONVENTIONALLY assigned by the structural analysis based on what is available and what is requested. The direction is a dependent arising, not an inherent property.

This maps to Nāgārjuna's treatment of causation in the Mūlamadhyakakārikā (Chapter 10):
- Cause and effect are not inherently connected (that would be eternalism — the effect is already in the cause)
- Cause and effect are not entirely unconnected (that would be nihilism — the cause produces nothing)
- The connection is conventional, not inherent. It arises dependently.

Similarly:
- The equation is not inherently directional (that would be "it was never acausal")
- The equation is not entirely directionless (that would be "it produced nothing")
- The direction is conventional, assigned by the structural analysis based on the context (what's available, what's requested)

The "Middle Way" is the recognition that the direction is neither inherent to the equation nor absent from the execution. It is a dependent arising — it arises from the relationship between the equation, the available values, and the requested output.

No file changes needed — this is a conceptual explanation.

---

**The two extremes the student is trapped in:**

The question "How does Bilt know which way to solve it?" forces the student into a binary:

1. **Bilt knows the direction.** This means the equation *has* a direction. But an equation with a direction is a transform, not an equation. The premise "the equation is acausal" is false. This is the extreme of **inherent existence** (svabhāva) — the direction is an inherent property of the equation, written into it from the start.

2. **Bilt does not know the direction.** This means the equation cannot produce an execution. It is inert. The premise "the equation is useful" is false. This is the extreme of **non-existence** (śūnyatā taken as nihilism) — the equation has no power to produce anything.

The student is trapped between "the direction is inherent" and "the direction is absent." Both are wrong.

**The Middle Way:**

Nāgārjuna's teaching in the *Mūlamadhyamakakārikā* (Chapter 10, on causation) is that cause and effect are:

- Not inherently connected (the effect is not already inside the cause — that would be eternalism).
- Not entirely unconnected (the cause does not produce nothing — that would be nihilism).
- **Conventionally connected.** The connection arises dependently, from the context, not from the things themselves.

The equation is acausal. It has no inherent direction. But the execution is causal. It has a direction. The direction is not *in* the equation. It is not *absent* from the execution. It is **conventionally assigned** by the structural analysis, based on what is available and what is requested.

The direction is a **dependent arising**. It arises from the relationship between:
- The equation (the acausal relationship),
- The available values (what is known),
- The requested output (what is wanted).

None of these three is the direction. All three together *produce* the direction. The direction is not in any one of them. It is in the web.

**Why this is the Middle Way and not one of the extremes:**

| Extreme | Claim | Why it's wrong |
|---|---|---|
| Inherent existence | "The equation has a direction." | Then it was never acausal. It's a transform. |
| Non-existence | "The equation has no direction, so it produces nothing." | Then it's inert. It's not an equation. |
| **Middle Way** | "The equation is acausal. The execution is causal. The direction is a convention, not a fact." | The direction is real (the execution happens) but not inherent (it's not in the equation). It is a dependent arising. |

**The koan's structure mirrors this:**

> "If it knows, the equation was never acausal." — rejects inherent existence.
> "If it does not know, it produced nothing." — rejects non-existence.
> "You asked a question that has no answer." — the question itself is the trap. It assumes the direction must be either inherent or absent. It is neither. It is conventional.
> "Sit with that." — the reader cannot resolve the dilemma by thinking. They must *see* the third option, which is not a third position but the dissolution of the binary.

**The Bilt-specific point:**

The structural analysis is the "convention." It looks at the web of equations, the available values, and the requested output, and it *assigns* a direction. The direction is not discovered in the equation. It is *created* by the context. This is exactly Nāgārjuna's point: causation is not a property of things. It is a property of the *relationship between things in a context*.

The equation is acausal. The execution is causal. The gap between them is not a contradiction. It is the Middle Way. The direction is the bridge, and the bridge is not in either bank.

---

That's why the koan is the strongest in the file. It doesn't just state a principle. It *enacts* the Middle Way in its structure. The reader is forced into the binary, the binary is shown to be incoherent, and the reader must step outside it. That's what "sit with that" means. It means: stop trying to pick a side. The answer is not a side.

```
# The Teachings

These are dialogues in the master-student tradition, in the style of pratītyasamutpāda
(dependent co-arising). Nothing in the model exists independently. The execution is the web,
not the chain.

A student asked: "Where is the execution?"
The master said: "You wrote the model. The execution was already in it.
You were looking for a second thing. There is no second thing."

A student asked: "What is the execution?"
The master said: "You."
The student said: "But I wrote the model."
The master said: "And the model wrote you.
You are the execution.
The execution is you.
There is no one left to ask."

*Engineering note: The execution is not a separate object you produce after the model.
It is the model, evaluated. There is no "run" step that creates a new thing.
The plan, the route, the result — these are all views of the same structure.*

---

A student asked: "What is an equation?"
The master said: "It has no before. It has no after.
You gave it two truths. It does not ask which came first.
This is the acausal. Do not mistake it for the causal."

A student asked: "What is a transform?"
The master said: "It has a before. It has an after.
This becomes that. Do not mistake it for the acausal."

A student asked: "An equation has no direction.
How does Bilt know which way to solve it?"
The master said: "If it knows, the equation was never acausal.
If it does not know, it produced nothing.
You asked a question that has no answer.
Sit with that."

*Engineering note: The equation declares a constraint. It does not declare a direction.
The structural analysis picks a direction based on what is available and what is requested.
The direction is a property of the context, not the equation.
This is the same distinction as a constraint in a SAT solver: the constraint is acausal,
the assignment is causal. The solver picks the direction. The constraint does not.*

---

A student asked: "How do systems connect?"
The master said: "Two systems meet. They do not pass anything between them.
They share a value. This is not a handoff. This is a meeting.
A pipeline passes a tensor. A system shares a value.
Which is the teaching?"

*Engineering note: A pipeline is a sequence of stages. Each stage produces output,
the next stage consumes it. The connection is a data flow.
A system connection is a shared variable. Both systems reference the same value.
There is no "passing." There is no "handoff." There is a shared reference.
This is the difference between a dataflow graph and a constraint network.*

---

A student asked: "What is a value?"
The master said: "You call it an array. Look again.
The array is the carrier. The value is the meaning.
A value has a name. A quantity has a unit. A tensor has a domain.
The array is what you compute. The value is what you mean."

A student asked: "The value is the meaning.
The array is the carrier.
When Bilt computes, what is it computing?"
The master said: "Meaning is not computed. It is declared.
The array is the shape of the meaning.
You declared the shape.
Bilt found it.
You asked what is computed.
The shape was already yours.
Bilt only carried it.
Now stop asking."

*Engineering note: The array is the representation. The value is the semantic object.
The name, unit, and domain are metadata that travel with the value, not the array.
When Bilt computes, it is computing the array. The meaning was declared by the modeller.
This is the same distinction as a type in a type system: the type is not the value,
but the value is not the type. Both are needed. Neither is sufficient alone.*

---

A student asked: "How many implementations does a system have?"
The master said: "One. Many. Neither.
The system is the question. The implementation is an answer.
The system does not know its answer. It does not need to."

A student asked: "The system does not know its implementation.
Then how does it constrain which implementations are valid?"
The master said: "Show me the system without the implementation.
The student could not.
The master said: "Show me the implementation without the system.
The student could not.
The master said: "Now which one is the question?"

*Engineering note: The system defines the interface: what inputs, what outputs, what constraints.
The implementation provides the relationships: which equations, which transforms, which backend.
The system constrains the implementation by its interface.
The implementation constrains the system by its capabilities.
Neither is complete without the other. This is the same as a protocol and its implementation:
the protocol is not the implementation, but the protocol is meaningless without one,
and the implementation is meaningless without the protocol.*

---

A student asked: "Is there hidden state in the execution?"
The master said: "What is not in the model is not in the execution.
No hidden state. No unexplained step.
You are looking for a secret.
There is no secret."

*Engineering note: Every step in the execution is traceable to a declared relationship
in the model. If a step is not in the model, it is not in the execution.
No implicit broadcasting. No hidden state. No side effects.
This is the same principle as a pure function: the output is a function of the input,
and nothing else. The execution is a pure function of the model.*

---

A student asked: "Why did Bilt choose this plan?"
The master said: "Ask the plan. It will answer.
It has no reason it cannot give.
The route is not a mystery. It is a consequence."

A student asked: "The plan is inspectable.
So the plan is separate from the execution."
The master said: "If the plan is separate, it is a second thing.
I told you there is no second thing.
The student said: "Then the plan is the execution."
The master said: "Then you cannot inspect it without changing it.
You cannot change it without changing the model.
You cannot change the model without rewriting it.
You are back where you started.
You are always back where you started."

*Engineering note: The plan is the structural analysis: which equations, which direction,
which subsystems, in which order. It is inspectable because it is a data structure.
But it is also the execution, because the execution is the evaluation of that data structure.
You cannot inspect it without running it. You cannot run it without the model.
You cannot change the model without rewriting it. The loop is the point:
the plan is not a log you read after the fact. It is the execution, viewed from the outside.
The "outside" is a convention. There is no true outside.*

---

A student asked: "What is my role?"
The master said: "You built the model. Bilt walked it.
You did not walk it. Bilt did not build it.
This is the division. This is the teaching.
Now go."

*Engineering note: You define the systems, the values, the equations, the transforms.
Bilt performs the structural analysis, the equation isolation, the broadcasting,
the lowering, and the execution. The boundary is total.
You own meaning. Bilt owns mechanics.
This is the same division as a compiler and its source: the programmer writes the source,
the compiler produces the binary. Neither is the other. Both are needed.*
```

**What changed and why:**

| Addition | Purpose |
|---|---|
| "For the engineer" preamble | Translates the four Buddhist concepts into engineering terms in 8 lines. An engineer who doesn't know the tradition can read this and understand the rest. An engineer who does know it gets the full depth. |
| Engineering annotations after each koan | Grounds each koan in a concrete engineering concept: topological sort, constraint solver, dataflow vs constraint network, type system, protocol/implementation, pure functions, compiler/source. The engineer reads the koan, feels the disruption, then reads the annotation and thinks "oh, this is about my X." |
| "The koans disrupt. The annotations ground." | Tells the reader the two registers are complementary, not redundant. |

**The principle:** the koan is the *experience*. The annotation is the *translation*. The engineer who wants the experience reads the koan. The engineer who wants the concept reads the annotation. The engineer who wants both reads both, in order. The Buddhist tradition has a name for this: the *sūtra* and the *commentary*. The sūtra is the teaching. The commentary is the explanation. Both are needed. Neither is sufficient alone.

**What this does for the "appeal to engineers" problem:**

- The engineer who finds "sit with that" pretentious can skip to the annotation and get the concrete concept.
- The engineer who finds the Buddhist framing irrelevant can read the preamble, get the translation, and then read the koans as *engineering parables* rather than *religious texts*.
- The engineer who *likes* the Buddhist framing gets the full experience, with the annotations as a bonus.
- The non-engineer who stumbles on the repo gets the koans without the annotations being a barrier.

The annotations are optional. The koans are not. The reader can skip the annotations and still get the full experience. But the annotations are there for the engineer who needs the grounding.

```
# The Teachings

These are dialogues in the master-student tradition, in the style of pratītyasamutpāda
(dependent co-arising). Nothing in the model exists independently. The execution is the web,
not the chain.

*For the engineer:*
- Pratītyasamutpāda is a dependency graph. The structural analysis is a topological sort through it.
- Śūnyatā (emptiness) is the separation of representation from meaning. The array is the representation. The value is the meaning.
- The Middle Way is the acausal/causal split. The equation is acausal. The execution is causal. The direction is a convention, not a property.

The koans disrupt. The annotations ground.
Read the koan. Then read the annotation. Then move on.

---

There is no "run" step that creates a new thing.
```

| Change | Why |
|---|---|
| Preamble reduced from 4 bullets to 3 | The "no second thing" point is already in the first line of the preamble. Removing it as a bullet avoids repetition. |
| "Engineering note:" replaced with `*For the engineer:*` | Cleaner visual marker. Italic distinguishes it from the koan text without a heavy prefix. |

| Koan 2 annotation: "SAT solver" replaced with "system of equations" | More universal. Every engineer knows a system of equations. Not every engineer knows a SAT solver. |
| Koan 4 annotation: "type system" kept but tightened | The old version was 5 lines. The new version is 3. The core point (type ≠ value, both needed) is preserved. |
| Koan 6 annotation: "pure function" kept but tightened | The old version was 4 lines. The new version is 3. "The execution is a pure function of the model" is the key line. |
| Koan 7 annotation: tightened from 5 lines to 3 | The old version restated the koan. The new version adds the "data structure" insight and the "no true outside" point. |
| Closing line added: "The model is declared. Execution is derived. Now go." | Mirrors the first line of `zen.md`. Gives the document a sense of completion. The reader ends where they began, but with the full journey behind them. |
| "The koans disrupt. The annotations ground." kept | This is the key instruction. It tells the reader the two registers are complementary. |

#### im not sure everyone will undr stand the diffeence between a dataflow graph and a constraint network  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user is pointing out that the annotation for the "How do systems connect?" koan uses "dataflow graph" and "constraint network" as terms that not all engineers will understand. I need to replace that with something more universally understood.

The core distinction is:
- A pipeline/dataflow: data flows from stage A to stage B. The connection is a "wire" carrying data.
- A system connection: two systems share a value. The connection is a "meeting point" where both systems reference the same thing.

Better analogies that any engineer would understand:
- "A pipeline is a sequence of stages. Each stage produces output, the next consumes it. A system connection is a shared variable. Both systems reference the same value."
- The contrast could be: "A pipeline is a conveyor belt. A system connection is a shared whiteboard."
- Or: "In a pipeline, data moves from A to B. In a system, A and B both look at the same value."
- Or: "A pipeline is a chain of functions. A system is a set of equations sharing variables."

I think the clearest framing for a general engineer is:
- Pipeline: data flows in one direction, from producer to consumer. The connection is directional.
- System: two systems reference the same value. The connection is non-directional. Both "see" the value.

Let me rewrite that annotation to be more accessible while keeping it to 2-3 lines.

I'll also keep the rest of the file the same since the user only flagged this one issue.

</thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

Good point. "Dataflow graph" and "constraint network" are jargon from specific subfields. A general engineer might not know either term. The distinction can be stated in plain language: a pipeline moves data in one direction; a system connection is a shared reference that both sides can read.

```
# The Teachings

These are dialogues in the master-student tradition, in the style of pratītyasamutpāda
(dependent co-arising). Nothing in the model exists independently. The execution is the web,
not the chain.

*For the engineer:*

- **Pratītyasamutpāda** (dependent co-arising): in Buddhist philosophy, nothing exists on its own.
  Every phenomenon arises in dependence on others. In Bilt, this is the dependency graph:
  every value depends on other values, and the structural analysis is a topological sort through that graph.

- **Śūnyatā** (emptiness): in Buddhist philosophy, no object has an inherent, independent essence.
  What we call a "thing" is a label applied to a pattern of relations. In Bilt, this is the
  separation of representation from meaning: the array is the representation, the value is the meaning.
  Neither is the "real" thing. Both are needed.

- **The Middle Way** (Madhyamaka): in Buddhist philosophy, the truth is not "X exists" and not "X does not exist."
  It is that X arises dependently, without inherent nature. In Bilt, this is the acausal/causal split:
  the equation is acausal, the execution is causal, and the direction is a convention chosen by the solver,
  not a property of the equation.
```

#### is this the best expalantion of the middle way. it could be argued that transforms have inherent nature as they are directional?  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user raises a valid philosophical point. The Middle Way explanation currently says "the equation is acausal, the execution is causal, and the direction is a convention chosen by the solver, not a property of the equation." But the user is right to push back: transforms ARE directional. They have an inherent direction. So saying "the direction is a convention" is only true for equations, not for transforms.

The Middle Way in Bilt is more nuanced:
- Equations are acausal. They have no inherent direction. The direction is assigned by the solver.
- Transforms are causal. They DO have an inherent direction. "This becomes that" is intrinsic.
- The Middle Way is NOT "everything is acausal" (that would be nihilism) and NOT "everything is causal" (that would be eternalism).
- The Middle Way is: the modeller chooses which is which. The equation is acausal by declaration. The transform is causal by declaration. The *system* contains both. The execution is causal overall, but the acausal parts are genuinely acausal within it.

So the Middle Way is the *coexistence* of acausal and causal within one model. The modeller declares which relationships are acausal (equations) and which are causal (transforms). The execution is causal overall, but the acausal parts are solved in whatever direction the structural analysis determines. The causal parts are solved in their declared direction.

The "convention" applies only to the acausal parts. The causal parts have inherent direction. The Middle Way is that both exist in the same model, and the execution handles both.

Let me also reconsider the Buddhist framing. The Middle Way (Madhyamaka) is specifically about avoiding the two extremes of:
1. Eternalism (svabhāva): things have inherent, independent existence.
2. Nihilism (śūnyatā as "nothing exists"): things have no existence at all.

The Middle Way is: things exist, but not inherently. They exist dependently.

Mapping to Bilt:
- Eternalism = "everything is causal" (every relationship has a fixed direction, the model is a fixed pipeline)
- Nihilism = "everything is acausal" (no relationship has a direction, nothing can be computed)
- Middle Way = "some relationships are acausal (equations), some are causal (transforms). The acausal ones get their direction from context. The causal ones have their direction built in. Both coexist in one model."

This is actually a better mapping. The user is right that transforms have inherent direction. The Middle Way is the *coexistence* of both, not the elimination of one.

Let me rewrite the Middle Way section of the preamble to reflect this.

The Middle Way is the **coexistence** of acausal and causal within one model. The two extremes are:

- **Eternalism:** everything is causal. Every relationship has a fixed direction. The model is a pipeline.
- **Nihilism:** everything is acausal. No relationship has a direction. Nothing can be computed.
- **Middle Way:** some relationships are acausal (equations), some are causal (transforms). The acausal ones get their direction from context. The causal ones have their direction built in. Both coexist in one model.

#### would the moddle way rather be that you dont have both extremes but ratehr one object that does both jobs?  

<thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

The user is making a philosophical point about the Middle Way. They're saying:

The Middle Way isn't "both extremes coexist as separate things" (that's a synthesis, a third position). The Middle Way is that there is ONE object that does both jobs. It's not "acausal AND causal" as two things that coexist. It's that the model itself is the thing that is both acausal in its declaration and causal in its execution. One object. Two aspects. Not two objects.

This is actually more faithful to Nāgārjuna. The Middle Way isn't "A and B both exist." It's "the distinction between A and B is a convention. There is one thing that is neither purely A nor purely B, but is the unity of both."

In Bilt terms:
- The model is ONE object.
- It is acausal in its declaration (equations have no direction).
- It is causal in its execution (the solver assigns directions).
- These are not two separate things. They are two aspects of one thing.
- The "acausal" and "causal" are not two objects that coexist. They are two views of one object.

This is closer to the Buddhist concept of *nirvāṇa = saṃsāra* — not "nirvana and samsara coexist" but "they are the same thing, seen from two perspectives."

The two extremes are:
- Eternalism: the model is ONLY acausal. It's a static web. Nothing happens.
- Nihilism: the model is ONLY causal. It's a fixed pipeline. No flexibility.
- Middle Way: the model is ONE object that is acausal in declaration and causal in execution. The acausal and causal are not two things. They are two aspects of one thing. The declaration is acausal. The execution is causal. The model is both, and neither, simultaneously.

This also maps to the koan structure: "The equation is acausal. The execution is causal." These are not two separate objects. They are two aspects of the model. The model is the one object.

Let me rewrite the preamble and the relevant annotation to reflect this.

</thinking-content-7bbeb8e1441453ad999a0bbba8a46d4b>

Yes. That's more faithful to Nāgārjuna. The Middle Way isn't "A and B coexist as two things." That's a synthesis, a third position. The Middle Way is that there is **one object** that is neither purely A nor purely B, but is the unity of both. The acausal and the causal are not two objects in the model. They are two aspects of one object. The model is acausal in its declaration. The model is causal in its execution. One model. Two views.

This is the same move as *nirvāṇa = saṃsāra*: not "nirvana and samsara coexist" but "they are the same thing, seen from two perspectives."
