| Location | Before | After | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Preamble, Middle Way | "The Middle Way is that both coexist. The modeller chooses which is which." | "The model is one object that does both. The declaration is acausal. The execution is causal. The model is both, and neither, simultaneously." | "Both coexist" implies two things. The Middle Way is one object with two aspects. |
| Preamble, Middle Way | "The two extremes are: everything is causal (a pipeline) or everything is acausal (nothing can be computed)." | "The two extremes are: the model is only acausal (a static web, nothing happens) or the model is only causal (a fixed pipeline, no flexibility)." | More precise. The extremes are about the *model*, not about "everything." |
| Koan 2 annotation | "The Middle Way is that both coexist in one model." | "But both are aspects of one model. The model is acausal in its declaration and causal in its execution. The acausal and the causal are not two objects. They are two views of one object." | "Two views of one object" is the correct Middle Way. "Both coexist" is a synthesis, not a Middle Way. |

The key philosophical correction: the Middle Way is not "A and B coexist." That's a third position, a compromise. The Middle Way is that there is **one object** that is neither purely A nor purely B. The model is one object. It is acausal in its declaration. It is causal in its execution. These are not two things in the model. They are two aspects of one thing. The distinction between "acausal" and "causal" is a convention we apply to the model, not a property of two separate objects.

The user is asking whether adding a pointer to Nāgārjuna for further reading would be a nice touch or too much. Let me think about this.

Arguments for:
- It gives the reader a path to go deeper if they're interested.
- It shows intellectual honesty — "this is not our invention, it's a tradition we're drawing from."
- It's a small addition (one or two lines).
- It mirrors the Buddhist tradition of pointing students to the source texts.
- It's the kind of thing that makes the document feel scholarly rather than performative.

Arguments against:
- It might feel like an afterthought, tacked on at the end.
- Most engineers won't read Nāgārjuna. It's a 2000-year-old Sanskrit text.
- It might make the document feel like it's trying too hard to be "deep."
- It could be seen as cultural appropriation if not handled carefully.
- The document is already long. Adding more might dilute the impact.

My recommendation: it's a nice touch IF it's done right. Not a "further reading" section (too academic), but a single line at the very end, after "Now go." — a quiet pointer. Something like "For the source: Nāgārjuna, Mūlamadhyamakakārikā, Chapter 10." It's one line. It's not a bibliography. It's a nod. It says "this is not our invention. Go to the source if you want." It's the Buddhist equivalent of a citation, but in the style of the document.

The key is placement and tone. It should be:
- After the final "Now go." (it's a postscript, not part of the teaching)
- One line, not a paragraph
- Quiet, not promotional
- Specific (chapter reference, not just "Nāgārjuna")

I'll suggest adding it and show the updated file.

# The Teachings

These are dialogues in the master-student tradition, in the style of pratītyasamutpāda
(dependent co-arising). Nothing in the model exists independently. The execution is the web,
not the chain.

*For the engineer:*

- **Pratītyasamutpāda** (dependent co-arising): in Buddhist philosophy, nothing exists on its own.
  Every phenomenon arises in dependence on others. In Bilt, this is the dependency graph:
  every value depends on other values, and the structural analysis is a topological sort through that graph.

- **Śūnyatā** (emptiness): in Buddhist philosophy, no object has an inherent, independent essence.
  What we call a "thing" is a label applied to a pattern of relations. In Bilt, this is the
  separation of representation from meaning: the array is the representation, the value is the meaning.
  Neither is the "real" thing. Both are needed.

- **The Middle Way** (Madhyamaka): in Buddhist philosophy, the truth is not "X exists inherently"
  and not "X does not exist at all." It is that X is one thing, seen from two perspectives.
  In Bilt, this is the model itself: one object that is acausal in its declaration
  and causal in its execution. The acausal and the causal are not two things in the model.
  They are two aspects of one thing. The two extremes are: the model is only acausal (a static web,
  nothing happens) or the model is only causal (a fixed pipeline, no flexibility).

**What was added:**

One line, after the closing "Now go.":

> *For the source: Nāgārjuna, Mūlamadhyamakakārikā, Chapter 10 (on causation).*

**Why this works:**

- **It's one line.** Not a bibliography. Not a paragraph. A pointer.
- **It's after "Now go."** The teaching is complete. The pointer is a postscript, not part of the teaching.
- **It's specific.** "Chapter 10" is the chapter on causation (*hetupratyayānupattivibhāga*). The reader who wants to go deeper has a starting point, not a vague "read Nāgārjuna."
- **It's quiet.** "For the source" is not "Further reading." It's not promotional. It's a nod. It says "this is not our invention. The tradition is older than us. Go to the source if you want."
- **It's the Buddhist equivalent of a citation.** A sutra often ends with "thus it was spoken" or "as the master said." This is the secular equivalent: "here is where this came from."

**Why it's not too much:**

The document is already long. One line at the very end, after the closing, is not an addition. It's a signature. It's the difference between a painting and a painting with the artist's name in the corner. The painting is complete without it. But the name is there, and it's right, and it doesn't intrude.
